And gave impartially;
Therefore you bear the mark between the brows,
And its radiant light is immeasurable.

Your eyes are like the new moon;
You behold every land of the ten directions without exception.
The mind's eye of all beings
Rejoices greatly in seeing you."

Then Śāriputra praised the Tathāgata with a hundred thousand verses, bowed his head at his feet, and circumambulated him a hundred thousand times. Then he said to the gods, nāgas, humans and nonhumans, "Sons of good family, alas, alas, the world is empty! Ah me, ah me, the world is empty! How sad, how sad, the world is empty! The boat and bridge of the precious true Dharma has fallen apart. The tree of unsurpassed awakening is broken. The precious sublime banner has fallen. The sun of the unsurpassed Buddha is setting behind the mountain of the great nirvāṇa."

When he had said this, the whole great assembly was struck with dread, so terrified that every hair on their bodies stood on end. The sun too lost its brightness, the mountains toppled, and the great earth shook violently.

Then Śāriputra spoke these verses to the great assembly:

"I have seen the body of the Buddha,
Like a mountain of refined gold.
His marks and qualities will all cease,
And only his name will remain.

To escape from the three worlds,
Strive with the power of diligence.
If you choose virtuous deeds,
Passing into nirvāṇa is the supreme bliss."

Then Śāriputra, to inspire the great assembly, displayed the power of his miraculous abilities. Through that power he rose into the sky above and emanated a great jeweled elephant with a thousand heads. He showed elephants entwined one with another, their thousand heads facing outward. Each of those elephants had seven tusks, and on each tusk there was a bathing pool. In each pool there were seven lotus flowers. On each lotus sat an emanated buddha. With each emanated buddha was a close disciple Śāriputra, and each Śāriputra sent forth great rays of light, illuminating world systems in the ten directions as numerous as the sands of the Ganges River, and gathered all those whose causes were in accord. When all those possessing the causes had assembled, Śāriputra displayed a great body that filled the whole of space. He also displayed his great body changing into a small one. He entered and emerged from the earth as if it were water, and also vanished from sight. He displayed fire blazing from the lower part of his body and water streaming from the upper part. Unobstructed in the sky above, he displayed countless miracles of every kind, from a hundred up to a thousand. Then he descended from the sky and taught the Dharma extensively to the great assembly, inspiring, encouraging and gladdening them. He led countless hundreds of thousands of beings to give rise to the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. He brought countless hundreds of thousands of beings to attain the fruits from stream-entry up to the fruit of arhatship, and further, he led countless hundreds of thousands of beings to set their minds on becoming śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas.

Having thus accomplished immeasurable benefit and happiness, Śāriputra said this to the great assembly:

"How shall I manage not to see the Tathāgata pass into nirvāṇa before me?" And rising into the sky above, he brought forth fire from his own body, burned his body, and passed into nirvāṇa.

At that time the members of the great assembly, stricken at heart, gazed at Śāriputra without blinking and wailed with loud cries, until their bodies were covered with dust. The sun too lost its brightness, and heaven and earth shook greatly. Then the great assembly gathered Śāriputra's bones, built a stūpa, and made offerings to it.

At that time hundreds of thousands in the great assembly, because they remembered Śāriputra, were tormented in mind by suffering, and grew dazed and bewildered until they lost their presence of mind. Then the Tathāgata, through the force of his compassion, emanated a Śāriputra and had him sit within the assembly. When the great assembly saw that Śāriputra, the suffering of their sorrow was stilled, and they became joyful once more. With their minds thus filled with joy, they all gave rise to the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening.

Then Ānanda, knowing in his mind through the blessing of the Tathāgata that the great assembly was in doubt, rose from his seat, arranged his upper robe over one shoulder, knelt with his right knee on the ground, joined his palms, bowed, and said to the Buddha, "Blessed One, for what cause and what condition did this Śāriputra pass into nirvāṇa before the Tathāgata, so that the great assembly is unhappy and grieving?"

The Blessed One said to Ānanda and the great assembly, "It is not only now, in this one instance, that Śāriputra has passed into nirvāṇa before the Tathāgata. In times past as well, unable to bear seeing me pass away first, he passed away first."

Then Ānanda said to the Buddha, "Blessed One, how was it that in times past Śāriputra passed away first?"

The Blessed One said, "Listen well. In times past, countless eons ago, at that time and in that age, there was a land called Vārāṇasī, and the king of the land of Vārāṇasī was called Great Radiance. The king called Great Radiance ruled over sixty petty kings of the borderlands and eight hundred cities. Since that king always had a mind of compassion, he gave generously to all, never going against anyone's wishes.

"At that time one of the petty kings of the borderlands disliked that king and constantly harbored ill will toward him. Every fortnight, on the six observance days, King Great Radiance loaded five hundred elephants with jewels, wealth, clothing, food and drink, heaped them up at the marketplaces and at the four gates of the city, and gave them away to all.

"At that time that hostile petty king heard, 'King Great Radiance gives to all, never going against their wishes, whatever they want, from clothing and food up to gold, silver and jewels.' Hearing this, that petty king of the borderlands grew jealous of the merit of generosity King Great Radiance was making. He gathered his ministers and commanded, 'Which of you can go and beg for the head of King Great Radiance of Vārāṇasī?' The ministers said, 'None of us is equal to it.'

"Then he proclaimed an edict to everyone: 'Whoever begs for the head of King Great Radiance of Vārāṇasī and comes back with it, I will give a thousand sealed measures of gold.' When he had proclaimed this edict, a brahmin said, 'I can go and beg for it. Prepare my provisions and supplies.' From that land to Vārāṇasī was a distance of six thousand krośas, so provisions and supplies were prepared accordingly, and the brahmin set out for Vārāṇasī.

"At that time, as that brahmin arrived in the region of the land of Vārāṇasī, the great earth quaked in six ways. The beasts of prey and the wild animals fled in terror in the four directions. The sun and moon grew dim and invisible, as if eclipsed. The five planets and the stars no longer moved in their proper seasons. Something like rainbows, red, black and white, appeared from time to time by day and by night. The stars were thrown into disorder and fell. The rivers, springs and bathing pools of that land, and its flourishing and lovely flowering and fruit trees, all dried up.

"Then that brahmin came to the gate of the palace of the king of the land of Vārāṇasī. At that time the god who guarded the palace gate said to the human gatekeeper, 'This wicked brahmin has come from far away to beg for the head of King Great Radiance. Do not let him in.' So the brahmin could not enter the palace. He waited and waited for seven days, and then said to the gatekeeper, 'I have come from far away and wish to meet the great king. Announce me.' Then the gatekeeper announced, as asked, 'A brahmin has come from far away and is now waiting at the gate.'

"As soon as the king heard these words, he came out to the gate, welcomed him, and greeted him as a father meets his son. He said, 'Where have you come from? Were you not troubled and wearied on the road?'

"The brahmin said, 'In another land I heard of your virtue, O king, of how you give without going against people's wishes. Your fair name is renowned throughout heaven and earth, and I heard that your virtue is no lie but true. So I too have made the journey on purpose over mountains and valleys, and I have come with a hope.'

"The king said, 'I am now called the one who gives everything. If you would ask, or if you have some need, ask without hesitation.'

"The brahmin said, 'Is that really true? I need nothing else at all. I am holding a great sacrifice, and so I have come to beg for the king's head.'

"When the king heard these words, he thought, 'From beginningless time I have circled through birth and death, and though I have wasted bodies like this one, it was never for the sake of the Dharma as it should be. Circling through birth and death to no purpose, I have been afflicted.

I have vowed to accomplish unsurpassed awakening with this body for the sake of all sentient beings, so if I were to break that vow, it would contradict my original resolve. And if I do not give this body, how shall I ever attain unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening?' Thinking this, he was overjoyed with the brahmin, and said, 'Let me give some brief instructions on the governing of the realm to the queens and the princes. Then in seven days I will give you what you want.'

"Then the great king went into the palace and addressed the queens: 'Between heaven and earth, all who are dear and beloved will be parted and separated. For humans, where there is birth there is death; where there can be creation, there can also be destruction. Whatever grows from the earth in summer all withers in winter.' So he spoke.

"When the queens and the princes heard these words the king had spoken, they were like a person choking, who can neither spit out nor swallow. They asked the king, 'Why do you say this?' The king said, 'A brahmin has come from far away to beg for my head, and I have agreed to give it.'

"When the queens and the princes heard these words, they threw themselves on the ground and wailed, tore their hair, and rent their clothes, and they said, 'Great king, if in the world nothing is dearer than one's own body, why do you give away and offer what is so very hard to give?'

"At that time the five hundred ministers also said to the brahmin, 'What do you want with this head, foul and filthy, full of pus and blood?' The brahmin said, 'I asked for it because I need it. Why should you question me?' The ministers said, 'You have come to our land, so we question you, and it is right that you answer properly.' Then, though the brahmin would have spoken plainly, he feared that the ministers would part him from his life, and in his fear he said nothing at all.

"Then the five hundred ministers said to the brahmin, 'Do not be afraid. We grant you safety.' Then they said to the brahmin, 'Poor brahmin, what use to you is that foul head of pus and blood? Each one of us five hundred ministers will exchange it for a head made of the seven precious substances, and we will also give you whatever you need, enough to last for seven generations.' The brahmin said, 'I do not want that.'

"The great ministers, their hopes unfulfilled, were tormented in mind with grief. They cried aloud, wept, and said to the king, 'Great king, for the sake of one brahmin you would abandon everything, the land, the subjects, the queens, the princes and the rest, and leave us orphaned forever. Can your heart bear to leave us behind?' The king said, 'I am giving away and offering this body for the sake of you and of all sentient beings.'

"At that time one of the great inner ministers heard what the king said and thought, 'Without doubt the king will give his body to this brahmin. It is a calamity.' And he thought, 'My heart cannot bear the king's giving up his body; I shall not look upon it.' He went into a secluded room, took up a sharp weapon, and cut off his own life.

"Then the great king went to the garden behind the palace, called the brahmin, and said, 'Come here. Now take my head. Out of love and affection for you, I will not turn aside your heart's wish. By this gift, may I in the future obtain the head of wisdom.' He joined his palms and bowed to the ten directions. Having bowed, he said, 'Buddhas and bodhisattvas of the ten directions, regard me with loving care, and bless me that this aspiration of mine may be fulfilled.' Then he said to the brahmin, 'Now I give it to you. Take it.'

"The brahmin said, 'The king has the strength of a mighty wrestler. Unable to bear the pain, he would turn and harm me. King, if you truly are able to do this, untie your hair yourself and fasten it to a tree.'

"When the king heard these words, a mind of still greater compassion arose in him, and he thought, 'This brahmin is thin and feeble. If he cannot cut off my head, my great aspiration will not be fulfilled.' So the king himself tied his topknot to the tree, and said to the brahmin, 'Cut off my head, and then place it back in my hands. With my own hands I will give it to you.'

"Then the brahmin took up a sword and crept toward him. At that moment the god of that tree struck the brahmin, and he fell senseless to the ground. Then King Great Radiance said to the god of the tree, 'Rather than helping me, will you turn and become an obstacle and a hindrance to my virtue?' Then the god of the tree, tormented with grief, cried, 'Alas, alas!' From a cloudless sky rain fell as blood, the great earth shook violently, and the sun lost its brightness and all grew dark. Then the brahmin cut off the king's head and went back to his own land.

"Then the five hundred princes and the ministers gathered the bones of King Great Radiance, built a stūpa, and made offerings to it."

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "The great inner minister of that time and that age, who could not bear to see King Great Radiance make the great gift of his head and so died first, is now Śāriputra. He who at that time and in that age was King Great Radiance is now I, the Tathāgata Śākyamuni. Thus the bodhisattva vowed to practice what is exceedingly hard to practice, and for the sake of all sentient beings repaid the kindness of the buddhas, and fully awakened into unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. Therefore Śāriputra, unable to bear seeing with his eyes my passing into nirvāṇa, passed into nirvāṇa first; and in times past too, unable to bear seeing me make that gift, he died first. Here in this garden behind the palace, at this very tree, I gave away heads as a wheel-turning king a full thousand times. What need is there, then, even to mention giving the limbs of the body, hands, feet and the rest?"

When the Buddha taught of these austerities he had performed, countless hundreds of thousands of sentient beings gave rise to the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening.

Countless hundreds of thousands of gods and humans attained the fruits from the fruit of stream-entry up to the fruit of arhatship. Countless hundreds of thousands of gods and humans set their minds on becoming śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas. When the Blessed One had spoken thus, the whole great assembly of gods, nāgas, yakṣas, humans, nonhumans and the rest rejoiced and departed.

Furthermore, it was like this. In the land of Magadha, five hundred bandits who lived among the mountain crags were constantly robbing, so that travelers could no longer go to and fro. At that time the king of the land of Magadha marshaled his fourfold army, overpowered the bandits, gouged out their eyes, cut off their ears and noses too, and cast them away among the steep mountain crags. At that time the five hundred bandits were tormented by the pain in their bodies and came close to death.

Among those five hundred bandits was one who had become a disciple of the Buddha. He said, "We are not far from death. Why do we not all go for refuge to the Buddha?" Then the five hundred bandits cried out with one voice, "Homage to the Buddha Śākyamuni!"

At that time the Tathāgata was staying on Vulture Peak. Through the force of his great compassion he went to a mountain called Gandha. A great wind arose, and on that mountain, where there was a grove of sandalwood trees, it raised a sandalwood powder that filled the whole expanse of the sky, and the sandalwood powder reached the mountain where the bandits were. That sandalwood powder settled on the bandits' wounds, and their eyes, their noses and all the wounds of their bodies were healed, and their blood too turned into milk. Then they all said, "Through the great compassion of the Buddha all the wounds of our bodies have been healed and we are at ease. To repay the Buddha's kindness, we set our minds on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening." Having said this, they all vowed with one voice, "We shall bring ordinary beings who have not attained happiness to attain happiness. We shall liberate those who are not liberated. We shall bring those who have not reached the far shore to the far shore. We shall bring those who have not attained the fruit to attain the fruit."

Furthermore, it was like this. The great compassion of the Tathāgata and his miraculous power of skillful means are inconceivable. When the Buddha was staying in Śrāvastī, five hundred bandits lived among the mountain crags, robbing travelers and doing what is contrary to the Dharma. At that time the Tathāgata, through the force of his skillful means, emanated himself as a man riding a fine elephant. He wore clothes on his body and carried a bow and arrows and weapons. The elephant was adorned with the seven precious substances, and his own body too was adorned with the seven precious substances, and he went alone along the side of the mountain crags where the bandits lived.

At that time the five hundred bandits saw such a man coming from afar, and said to one another, "In all our many years as bandits we have never seen anything like this."

At that time the bandits had a leader, and he asked, "What have you seen?" They said, "There is a man riding a fine elephant coming this way. That man's clothes and ornaments are made of the seven precious substances, and the elephant too is adorned with the seven precious substances. Someone like that, shining over heaven and earth, is coming straight along the road. If we strike that man and seize him, we will have riches enough to last seven generations."

At that time, when the bandit leader heard these words, he was overjoyed, and he planned it thus: "Let us creep up slowly, and when we have come near, all of us shoot arrows and seize him." Then the five hundred bandits approached him and, all at once, raised a great shout.

At that time the emanated man, with a mind of love and compassion, drew his bow and shot, and each of the five hundred bandits was struck by one arrow. Suffering unbearable pain, they rolled on the ground, cried aloud and wept. When they tried to pull the arrows out, the arrows were lodged so fast that, though they strained with all the strength they had, they could not do it. Then the five hundred bandits were terrified and thought, "Now we shall surely die." Why? "It is a great marvel that all of us together could not overcome a single man." And all with one voice they spoke thus:

"Among humans, what are you?
A god, a nāga, or a hungry ghost?
Or is it the power of a spell?
By one arrow shot, five hundred are wounded.
The suffering of this pain is hard to bear;
For our lives' sake we beg of you,
Please draw out these venomous arrows.
From now on we shall not offend."

Then the emanated man spoke in verse:

"No wound goes beyond the wound of the axe;
Among missiles the short arrow is supreme.
Youth and a wrestler's strength cannot remove it;
Through much learning it comes to be no more."

At that time, having spoken this verse, the emanated man changed back into the body of a buddha and sent forth great rays of light, illuminating all the world systems of the ten directions. Blind beings touched by that light came to see forms with their eyes. The lame and the hunchbacked were straightened again. Those whose bodies lacked hands or feet came to possess all their limbs. The insane too regained their presence of mind. In short, everything that was unpleasant became pleasant.

At that time the Tathāgata taught the five hundred bandits the Dharma in many ways, inspiring, encouraging and gladdening them. When the five hundred bandits heard the Dharma they were overjoyed; the wounds of their bodies were healed and their blood turned into milk. They set their minds on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, and all with one voice they offered these verses:

"Having set our minds on awakening,
We shall bring vast benefit to beings.
We shall train in the Buddha's teaching
And always offer reverence and service.

Protected by the force of the Buddha's compassion,
Freed from suffering, our minds and bodies are at ease.
The buddhas and the bodhisattvas
And our spiritual friends: we shall remember their kindness.

Teachers, masters, fathers and mothers,
And all other sentient beings,
Friend and foe alike, we shall regard with an equal mind.
The merit of repaying kindness has no equal."

At that time, in the sky above, the gods of the desire realm, Śakra, lord of the gods, and the rest, let divine flowers fall like rain, made offerings to the Tathāgata with divine music, and with one voice offered these verses:

"Through our merit of the past
Our light and radiance are supremely beautiful.
With sublime articles of offering
We act for the benefit of all.

To meet a buddha is exceedingly hard;
To hear the true Dharma is extremely hard.
Because in former times we made roots of virtue,
We have met the god of the Śākyas.

To remember the Buddha's kindness
We set our minds on awakening.
Our seeing of the Buddha
And the virtues of the three vehicles
We dedicate wholly to unsurpassed awakening
For the sake of all beings without exception."

At that time, having spoken these verses, the gods circumambulated him a hundred thousand times, bowed their heads to the Buddha, and went back up into the sky.

Furthermore, it was like this. The power of the Tathāgata's skillful means and of the roots of virtue of his great compassion is inconceivable. At that time, in the land of Vaiśālī, there was a brahmin who clung to wrong views and was wholly in the grip of pride. Śāriputra and Mahāmaudgalyāyana befriended that brahmin and often went to his house and taught him the Dharma, but he did not gain faith and remained attached to his evil views.

That brahmin was very rich and had a great many precious jewels, but he had no son. He thought, "If I die, all my wealth and livestock will be taken back." Then he made offerings and prayers to the gods of the sacred mountains, the gods of the trees and others like them, and after ninety days his wife became pregnant. When the months were complete she gave birth to a boy, handsome, lovely to behold, bearing the marks of a fine man, dear to his parents' hearts, and pleasing to many.

They raised that son, and when he was twenty years old he went wandering outside with his friends to see the shows. On the road he met a plowing ox, was trampled by it, and died. When the parents heard that their son had died, they cried aloud, wept and wailed, rolled on the ground, covered their bodies with dust, and tore their hair, saying, "In a single morning my treasure is gone!" Wailing, they went to where their son's corpse lay, took the corpse in their arms, and wept until they fainted and came to again, until they were out of their minds. They cast off their clothes and went about naked, and so they met the Tathāgata.

Then the Tathāgata, through the force of his great compassion, emanated their son and let them meet him. The boy's parents were filled with great joy. They took the boy in their arms, their madness cleared, and their minds returned to them, and the Tathāgata taught them the Dharma. When the brahmin heard the Dharma, at once he gave rise to the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening.

Furthermore, it was like this. The power of the Tathāgata's great compassion and roots of virtue is inconceivable. At that time King Vaiḍūrya marshaled his fourfold army, defeated the people of Kapilavastu, and seized the sons of the Śākyas. He dug pits in the earth as deep as their armpits and buried them in the ground so that they could neither stir nor move. When they had been buried in the ground like this for seven days, the Tathāgata, through the power of his great compassion, transformed the pits into bathing pools. The water of the pools had the eight qualities, and lotus flowers covered their surface: padma and puṇḍarīka lotuses, blue, red, yellow and white, as large as chariot wheels, covered them completely. He also emanated birds of many kinds singing sweetly. At that time, when this happened, the sons of the Śākyas were filled with great joy, and at once they gave rise to the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening.

When they had set their minds on awakening, King Vaiḍūrya made five hundred fierce black elephants drunk with liquor and maddened, shod their feet with iron hooves, tied sharp swords to their trunks, and, raising evil cries, made them trample the sons of the Śākyas. The hooves of the beasts trampled the sons of the Śākyas and left their joints, flesh, skin and bones all ground into a filthy pulp on the earth.

Through the power of the Tathāgata's great compassion they felt no distress in mind or body. Because body and mind were at ease, they gave rise to the mind of awakening. Because they had given rise to the mind of awakening, they came to have an even, impartial mind toward all sentient beings. Because their minds were impartial, they had no thought of hatred. Because they had no hatred, when they passed from that life they were reborn as gods in the heavens.

Having been reborn as gods in the heavens, they examined with the divine eye from what cause this had first come about, and they said to one another, "Through the kindness of the Buddha's great compassion we have been born as gods. We have mansions of the seven precious substances and sublime divine garments, our bodies have light, and we possess music and every enjoyment of bliss. Since this has come about through the miraculous power of the Tathāgata, we too shall give rise to the mind of great compassion for the benefit and happiness of all sentient beings. Wherever the Buddha's Dharma is, whether in a village, a town, on a mountain, in a grove, at the foot of a tree, in a palace compound or in a house, wherever this sūtra is studied, read, recited or explained, in that place we shall provide and offer whatever is wished, so that the Dharma is never interrupted. In that place we shall guard and protect it day and night, so that harm from weapons, epidemics and famine do not arise."

When they had made this aspiration, the life span, strength, bodily complexion and luminosity of those gods became even finer than before, and in great joy they departed back through the sky.

Furthermore, the great compassion of the Tathāgata's skillful means and the power of his roots of virtue are inconceivable. At that time King Vaiḍūrya, after he had conquered the land of Kapilavastu and wiped out the sons of the Śākyas, chose five hundred daughters of the Śākyas, beautiful and more accomplished in every art than others. Surrounded by these five hundred young women and with music playing, he returned to his own land. Gathering together with his queens and consorts in the palace, he sat down cross-legged. Seated on his throne, he said, "I am happy! Excellent!"

Then the daughters of the Śākyas said to King Vaiḍūrya, "Why are you happy?"

The king said, "I am happy because I have triumphed over my enemies."

The young women said, "You have not triumphed. Even if the king's entire fourfold army had risen up, it could not have overcome a single son of the Śākyas. Because we Śākyas have become disciples of the Buddha and do not contend with anyone, the king has merely won. Did you not, having given rise to evil intent, lead your fourfold army against Kapilavastu three or four times, one after another, and were you not always defeated? When you first led your army there, the sons of the Śākyas said this: 'If we fight with this shameless, ungrateful King Vaiḍūrya who comes to harm us, there will be no distinction at all between the wise and the foolish, and neither white nor black will be evident. Let us merely frighten him so as to turn him back.' And they all vowed thus: 'We shall shoot our arrows all at once, but we shall not wound or harm the other side.' Then they arrayed their fourfold army and met your army forty krośas out in front, and when they all shot their arrows the stream of arrows was unbroken. King, did you not take fright and turn back?

"Then again, after ninety days had passed, you once more led your fourfold army against the Śākyas and attacked. At that time the sons of the Śākyas deliberated thus: 'This evil King Vaiḍūrya, who knows neither shame nor embarrassment, has now come again to harm us, so we Śākyas make this vow: we shall shoot our arrows at those enemies all at once, without harming their bodies, cutting all the lacings of their armor so that their armor falls loose.' Having vowed this, all the Śākyas shot their arrows, cut all the lacings of the armor, and left them naked. Then, prince, you were frightened and deliberated, 'If it comes to our lives, what shall we do?' Did not one minister then give this counsel: 'The Śākyas are disciples of the Buddha, so they keep the precept of not taking life, and because their compassion is great they did not harm us. Were it otherwise, our bodies and lives would be gone'? When he had said this, you said, 'If that is true, then attack still further.' At that time the sons of the Śākyas remained without moving a hand. Though King Vaiḍūrya's fourfold army drew near, the sons of the Śākyas remained without fighting back.

"Then a brahmin who was there said to the Śākyas, 'Now that disaster has come, why do you remain without fighting back?' The Śākyas said, 'We do not contend with anyone. If we were to contend with this, we would not be worthy to be disciples of the Buddha.' Then that brahmin, displeased with what they had said, stepped forward alone in front of the Śākyas and attacked King Vaiḍūrya's army. Each arrow he shot wounded seven men at a time, so that before long many were wounded and killed. King, did you not turn back with your fourfold army? After the army had turned back, the Śākyas thought, 'It is not right for us to stay and keep company with this brahmin.' With this thought the Śākyas assembled and expelled the brahmin from that land. It was after that brahmin had been expelled that the king again led his fourfold army, conquered the land of Kapilavastu, and won."

Then King Vaiḍūrya was abashed and ashamed. He summoned low-caste butchers, had the hands and feet of the daughters of the Śākyas chopped off and their noses and ears cut off, carted them away, and threw them into a charnel ground. Then the daughters of the Śākyas, their hands and feet cut off, rolled about on the ground, experiencing great suffering, and were at the point of death. Tormented by suffering, some of the daughters of the Śākyas called out the names of their fathers and mothers, brothers and sisters; some called upon heaven; some called upon the earth; some cried out in utter misery.

Among them, one who was foremost among the daughters of the Śākyas said to the young women, "Sisters, know this. Once I heard this from the Buddha: I heard that whoever is destitute and in distress, if they single-mindedly bring the Buddha to mind and recite his name, then as their life ebbs away they will accomplish what they wish and will come to happiness."

Then the five hundred daughters of the Śākyas bowed with one-pointed minds and said in unison, "Homage to the Buddha Śākyamuni, the arhat, the perfectly and completely awakened Buddha!" And they cried out, "Alas, alas! Oh, how wretched! Oh, how wretched! Blessed One, Sugata!"

The moment they had said this, through the force of the Tathāgata's great compassion, a cloud of great compassion gathered in the sky above. Rain fell from that cloud of great compassion, and the raindrops struck the young women's bodies. As soon as the rain touched them, their hands, feet and all their other limbs were restored just as they had been before. Then the daughters of the Śākyas were overjoyed, and all of them said with one voice, "The Tathāgata is our loving father and mother! The unsurpassed Blessed One is the sublime medicine of the world! He is the eye that gives the world light! He is the one who liberates from the suffering of the three realms! He is the giver of supreme bliss!"

"Why do we say this? Because we have been freed from the torment of suffering. In order to remember the Buddha's kindness, if we now wish to repay the Buddha's kindness, by what means shall we repay it? The body of the Tathāgata is a vajra body, an everlasting body, a body without hunger or thirst, a body of sublime form. He is endowed with the inconceivable perfection of the meditative concentrations, the samādhis, the faculties, the powers and the limbs of awakening. He is fully adorned with the thirty-two major marks and the eighty excellent minor marks. He abides in the great parinirvāṇa and regards all sentient beings as he regards Rāhula. He regards friend and foe alike, and hopes for no reward. If we now wish to repay the Buddha's kindness, let us go forth, keep moral discipline, and uphold the sublime Dharma." With this thought they sought robes and alms bowls and went to a nunnery to request ordination.

When we arrived at the nuns' dwelling, we first met the group of six nuns, and the group of six nuns admonished us thus: "Girls, you are young and in the bloom of youth, lovely and beautiful. Why do you think of giving up what is so hard to give up, and think of going forth? Would you not be happier enjoying the five sense pleasures of the world? Once you have known happiness and reached old age, would it not be soon enough to go forth?" So they admonished us. The six nuns had thought, "If these girls do not go forth, they will surely give us their robes, their alms bowls and everything they own," and with this thought they obstructed the daughters of the Śākyas from going forth.

When the young women heard these words they became all the more unhappy and were overcome with grief. "Why must we meet with yet another great danger such as this? Just as there may be deadly poison in fine food, so too are these nuns. Even we know that the five sense pleasures of the world are full of the faults of danger, so why do these nuns see them as good qualities?" With this thought they cried out aloud and wept.

Then we came away, and as we went along we met the nun Utpalavarṇā, who asked us, "Why are you weeping?"

We said, "We are weeping because things with the nuns did not turn out as we had hoped."

Utpalavarṇā said, "What did you hope for that has not come about?"

"We asked to be ordained, but we were not permitted to go forth."

Utpalavarṇā said, "If you wish to go forth, I shall ordain you."

The daughters of the Śākyas were overjoyed and went forth as disciples of Utpalavarṇā. Having gone forth in this way, they all gathered and talked together, saying, "Before we went forth, we did not merely experience a little suffering. Our fathers, mothers, relatives and friends were killed by the enemy, and our noses, ears, hands and feet were all cut and chopped off. We experienced inexhaustible, unimaginable suffering."

Utpalavarṇā said, "What is there to talk about in suffering only that much? It does not compare with the account of the suffering I experienced before I went forth."

The daughters of the Śākyas joined their palms and said to their preceptor, "Please tell us what kind of suffering you experienced when you had not yet gone forth."

Then the nun Utpalavarṇā entered samādhi and, by the might of her higher perception, sent forth great rays of light that illuminated Jambudvīpa. She gathered gods, nāgas, yakṣas, humans, nonhumans and others who were linked to her by karma and concordant causes, and to that great assembly she spoke thus:

"Before I went forth, I was living in the land of Śrāvastī, and my father and mother gave me as a bride to a man who lived in the north. According to the custom of the land of those northerners, when a woman was about to give birth she was sent back to her parents' land to give birth. In this way I gave birth over many years, one child after another. Later, when I was about to give birth to another child, I set out for my parents' land together with my husband, with horses and a carriage for our conveyance. When we had gone halfway, there was a river in flood, and we could not cross it, so that night we slept on the riverbank in a lonely pasture.

"That night I gave birth, and a venomous snake in the grass caught the smell of blood and came toward where I had given birth. Before it reached me, it came upon my husband and a servant who were sleeping, and it bit them, killing the servant first. Then it came upon my husband. I called to him, 'A snake is coming! A snake is coming!' but he did not hear, and my husband too was killed. Then it killed the horses, the oxen and all the beasts of burden. I alone escaped. The next day, when dawn broke and I looked, I saw my husband's corpse swollen and the blood lying on the ground like mire. I was terrified and fell to the ground. Then I cried out loudly and wept, beat my breast with my fists, tore out my hair, and fell upon the corpse.

"For many days in this way I could not get across the water and stayed on the riverbank. Later, when the flood had subsided, I left the eldest child on the near bank, carried the middle child on my back, and put the youngest in the fold of my garment. Holding the fold in my teeth, I waded into the water. When I had gone halfway across, I looked back at my children and saw that a tiger had come and seized the eldest. I opened my mouth and screamed, and the fold of my garment slipped away, and the youngest fell into the water. As I grabbed for the youngest, the child on my back also slipped off, and all my children died. So one child was eaten by the tiger and the others died in the water. I reached the far side, so tormented by suffering that my heart all but split, and I vomited blood from my mouth. I wailed, 'Alas, alas!' and sat weeping, saying, 'In a single day I have come to see suffering such as this!'

"Before long a large party of travelers arrived there. Among those travelers was a householder who was a close friend of my parents. I went to him and asked for news of my parents. The householder said this to me: 'Fire broke out in your parents' house, and the house burned down. Your father and mother were burned as well.' When I heard these words, I fainted and fell to the ground."

"Then, after a long while, I came to again and went on, and as I traveled, my companions met with five hundred bandits and were overcome. At that time the bandit chief took me as his wife and always made me keep watch at the door. When he set me to keep watch at the door, he commanded me, 'If there is any emergency, keep a sharp lookout and let me in.'

"Then, at another time, that husband went off with the bandits to rob and plunder, and he was pursued by the people of the king's city. In distress he fled back home, and he arrived just at the time when I was giving birth. Although I heard my husband calling at the door, I could not open it. At that time the husband thought, 'This wife of mine must be plotting to harm me; she will not even open the door.' With this thought he climbed over the wall and came inside. He said to his wife, 'Why do you not open the door?' The wife said, 'I could not open the door because I was giving birth.'

"Then that husband grew angry and commanded, 'As long as there is a body, there will be children. Since you have harmed me, even though you have this child, no matter what, kill it at once!' At that time I loved the child too much and could not bring myself to kill it. Then my husband drew his sword, cut off the child's hands and feet, and said, 'Eat this flesh! If you do not eat the flesh, I will cut off your head!' Then, terrified, I ate the child's flesh. When I had eaten the child's flesh in this way, his anger subsided.

"Then, at yet another time, he went off to steal and plunder, was caught by the king, and was put to death according to the law. At that time, by the custom of that king's land, when a thief had been killed, the thief's corpse and his wife, still alive, were placed in a tomb. Others saw that I had been placed in the tomb together with my ornaments, and soon afterward someone who wanted my ornaments dug open the tomb at night, took me away as well, and made me his wife. Before long that tomb robber too was caught by the king and executed according to the law. By the custom of the land, the thief's wife was again placed alive in the tomb with the corpse. Then, before long, hungry wolves ate the corpse and dug their way out, and I too escaped from there.

"Bewildered as though drunk, I wandered on and met a large crowd of people, and I said to them, 'I am tormented by a great many sufferings, and now I long for a place of happiness free from sorrow.' Then a brahmin householder, out of compassion for me, said this to me: 'I have heard that if one enters the Dharma of the Buddha Śākyamuni, there are no conditions for the afflictions, and one is utterly happy.' When I heard these words, a mind of great joy arose in me. I went to where the nuns, the great Prajāpatī and Yaśodharā, were, and when I arrived I went forth. Having gone forth, I meditated step by step and attained the fruit, and came to possess the three knowledges, the six higher perceptions and the eight liberations. Through that cause and that condition, before I went forth I experienced such great suffering, and having experienced it, through that condition I went forth and also attained the fruit."

At that time, when the daughters of the Śākyas heard her say this, a mind of great joy arose in them, and they obtained the stainless Dharma eye with regard to the Dharma. The assembly listening to the Dharma also made aspirations for awakening and departed in joy.

At that time the Buddha's aunt and foster mother, the nun Gautamī, said this to the nuns, the probationary nuns, the novices, the laywomen and all women: "The Buddha's Dharma possesses the qualities that bring benefit and happiness to all. In order to attain the three kinds of fruit, all sentient beings have the fortune to enter the ocean of the Tathāgata's Dharma, but it has not been granted to us women. That is because all women have many obscurations; they cannot let go, and are attached and clinging; they are open to countless thoroughly afflicted actions; their understanding is small and they cling; they are lazy and very proud; with this very body they cannot become a cause of awakening, and they are not fit to attain the thirty-two major marks; while wandering in saṃsāra they cannot attain the supreme fruit of a wheel-turning king and gather many beings by the Dharma of the ten virtues; they do not attain the unsurpassed means of a king of Brahmās, and cannot urge the turning of the wheel of the sublime Dharma for the benefit and happiness of all sentient beings. Therefore the Tathāgata has not permitted women to become disciples who enter the Dharma. Just as divine māras, those with evil views and all the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) are forever attached to and clinging to evil views, and wish to destroy the sublime Dharma and to overthrow the Buddha, the Dharma and the Saṅgha, so likewise the Tathāgata has not permitted women to enter, even though they have faith in the Buddha's Dharma.

"For the sake of all women I asked the Tathāgata again and again, up to three times, for permission to enter the Buddha's Dharma, but he did not grant it. Though I asked up to three times in this way, he did not grant it, and since things did not turn out as I had hoped, I was overcome by unhappiness and grief. With my eyes full of tears I came away, and as I went along I met Ānanda on the road. Ānanda asked me, 'Mother, why are you so unhappy and grieving?' At that time I said this to Ānanda: 'I have faith and devotion in the Buddha's Dharma, and I asked the Tathāgata up to three times, but he did not grant it. That is why I am unhappy and grieving.' When I had said this, Ānanda said to me, 'Mother, do not be unhappy. I shall ask the Tathāgata, and I shall bring you, Mother, into the Buddha's teaching.' Then at once I became joyful.

"Then Ānanda went to where the Buddha was, and when he arrived he said this: 'Blessed One, if you grant me the opportunity to ask a question, I shall ask the Blessed One.' The Blessed One declared, 'Ask whatever you wish.' Then Ānanda said this to the Blessed One:"

"Mother Gautamī nourished and raised the Tathāgata's form body, and that he has now become fully awakened is also because of his mother as a condition that he became perfectly and completely awakened. Since his mother has been so kind to the Tathāgata, and the Tathāgata permits even all sentient beings to enter the Buddha's teaching, what need is there to speak of his mother entering the Buddha's teaching?"

The Blessed One declared to Ānanda, "It is as you say. It is not that the Tathāgata does not know how kind his mother has been to the Tathāgata, yet he has not permitted women to enter the Buddha's teaching. If the Tathāgata were to permit women to enter the Buddha's teaching, the sublime Dharma would decline five hundred years earlier. Therefore the Tathāgata is not pleased to have women enter the Buddha's teaching."

Then Ānanda bowed his head at the Buddha's feet, joined his palms, bowed, and again said this to the Blessed One: "As I clearly recall, all the buddhas of the past had the fourfold assembly. Will not the Buddha Śākyamuni alone have the fourfold assembly?"

The Buddha declared to Ānanda, "If Gautamī has faith in the Buddha's Dharma in this way, then if she gives rise to great diligence and practices the eight rules of respect in complete purity, she is permitted to enter the Buddha's Dharma."

Then Ānanda bowed his head at the Buddha's feet, circumambulated him three times, went back, and said this to his mother: "I earnestly entreated the Tathāgata, and you, Mother, are permitted to enter the Dharma."

"Most excellent! Ānanda, it is excellent that you entreated the Tathāgata again and again, so that things have turned out as your mother hoped." When his mother heard from Ānanda that she was permitted to enter the Tathāgata's teaching, she was overjoyed: "Today, here, this impermanent body of mine has become a permanent, precious body. This life of mine too, which changes and passes away from moment to moment, has today, here, become a precious life. Until now my body, my life and my wealth have existed merely from the coming together of conditions, but now I have found precious wealth." Seeing in this way great qualities of benefit and happiness, she gave rise to a mind to honor and serve Ānanda all the more, and she said this to Ānanda: "Have no doubt. I shall practice in accordance with what the Tathāgata has intended and taught, and even if it costs me my body and life, I shall not let it lapse." Then the Tathāgata also explained in detail how the eight rules of respect are not to be allowed to lapse.

At that time Mother Gautamī, by the force of great compassion, for the sake of all women of the future, again said this to the Blessed One: "Venerable Blessed One, if in the future there should be a woman who has faith and devotion in the Buddha's Dharma, please permit her as well to enter the teaching."

The Blessed One declared, "Excellent! Any woman who protects and upholds the Buddha's Dharma and gradually takes up moral discipline, generosity, much learning and the virtuous dharmas is permitted to go forth; and even if she remains a householder, she is permitted to receive the three refuges, the five precepts and up to the full precepts, and to enter into liberation and on up to the dharmas of the limbs of awakening. If she strives earnestly in these, she will also attain the three kinds of fruit, and will attain even the nirvāṇa of gods and humans."

When Gautamī heard him say this, she was overjoyed, delighted and glad at heart, and she said this to the Blessed One: "That I have attained the fruit is also the kindness of the Buddha."

The Blessed One declared, "Do not say so. It is not that the Tathāgata has shown kindness to sentient beings. The Tathāgata does not think, 'I have been kind to sentient beings,' or 'I have not been kind.' If the Tathāgata were to count up his kindnesses to sentient beings, his mind of equanimity would be impaired. Gautamī, if the Tathāgata were to think, 'I have been kind to sentient beings,' or 'I have not,' there would be no equanimity at all. Why is that? Even if some sentient being does harm to the Buddha, there is no aversion toward that being; and even if some sentient being anoints the Tathāgata's body with a paste of incense and sandalwood, the Tathāgata does not think of being pleased with that being. The Tathāgata regards sentient beings with equanimity. This is Ānanda's kindness, not the Tathāgata's kindness, for it is through Ānanda as a condition that women have come to enter the Buddha's teaching. Gautamī, in the future, nuns, laywomen and women should always keep Ānanda in mind single-mindedly. They should honor and serve him greatly, recite his name, and constantly remember his kindness. Or else they should not forget him in the six periods of the day and night; they should remember him."

Then Gautamī instructed the nuns and the women thus: "We take refuge in our teacher Ānanda for as long as we live. Any woman who wishes always to be happy and content, to have good fortune and to attain the fruit should always, on the eighth day of the middle month of spring and on the eighth day of the middle month of autumn, put on new, clean clothes, single-mindedly take the eight precepts for one day and night, and, rousing diligence, make offerings during the six periods. Then Ānanda will protect her through the might of his miraculous power and bring about whatever she wishes."

The great assembly, having heard the Dharma, was overjoyed, circumambulated, and departed.

This concludes the chapter called Compassion, the seventh, from The Great Skillful Means by Which the Buddha Repays Kindness.

The Great Skillful Means by Which the Buddha Repays Kindness, fascicle six.

At that time the Tathāgata was surrounded by a great assembly, who made offerings and paid him service, showed him honor and reverence, and sat praising and extolling him. Then Ānanda, perceiving with his mind the doubts of the great assembly, rose from his seat and said this to the Buddha:

"Blessed One, why did you permit even someone of low birth and base caste, such as Aniruddha, to go forth? Because Aniruddha has gone forth, the minds of faith and respect of all the royal caste and the merchant caste have declined. Because their minds of faith have declined in this way, their grounds of merit have entirely declined. Even the monk Nanda, son of King Śuddhodana, has given rise to a contemptuous attitude."

Then the Blessed One declared this to Ānanda and the great assembly: "Listen well. It would be easier for you to say that the Tathāgata lacks the great compassion of equanimity, the three close applications of mindfulness, the five wisdoms and samādhi, than to say that the monk Aniruddha is truly of low birth and base caste, or of inferior conduct, inferior aspiration or inferior diligence. Can that be said? It would be easier for you to call the Tathāgata afflicted, impermanent, subject to change and passing away, but it is not right to say that the monk Aniruddha went forth from a low birth and base caste. Since the Tathāgata speaks words of genuine truth through the wisdom of omniscience, you should trust what the Tathāgata has said.

"The Tathāgata, seeing and knowing the faults and flaws of saṃsāra, became perfectly and completely awakened by himself. Through going forth, one such as the monk Aniruddha has come to possess the three knowledges, the six higher perceptions and the eight liberations, and has become an object of offering for gods and humans. He has become supreme and sublime as a protector of the sublime Dharma and an upholder of the Vinaya. Because he has become an object of offering for all, even ordinary beings come to attain the three sublime fruits through him, namely: they attain a fruit in the present, a fruit in their next birth and a fruit in the future. Therefore Aniruddha possesses supreme and sublime conduct; he is a bodhisattva of utterly great compassion. In times past, too, he generated roots of virtue with and paid homage to as many as hundreds of thousands of koṭis of countless buddhas, and in the Dharma of those buddhas too he was foremost among the upholders of the Vinaya. Now, in the Dharma of the Buddha Śākyamuni as well, he has become foremost among the upholders of the Vinaya."

At that time, when the monk Nanda had heard what the Buddha said, he rose from his seat, bowed his head at the feet of the noble Kauṇḍinya, and went on down the line to Aniruddha, where he merely joined his palms and gave a slight bow. Then the Blessed One spoke to Nanda in verse:

"Nanda, you should understand this:
One does not become poor and wretched,
Nor does one's greatness decline;
This is the very nature of going forth."

When the Blessed One had instructed Nanda in this way, encouraged him and spoken many kinds of delightful Dharma, Nanda was overjoyed and delighted. He arranged his robe and bowed his head at the feet of the noble Aniruddha, and at that very moment heaven and earth shook in six ways. His body and mind became pliant and workable; he fully attained his own aim and did what had to be done.

Then the Blessed One said to the noble Aniruddha, "Quickly roar your lion's roar! That is, teach the Three Jewels, the four truths, the distinctions among the seven assemblies of householders and renunciants, their three refuges, and the moral disciplines from the five precepts up to all the moral disciplines: the moral discipline of benefiting sentient beings, the moral discipline of purifying the afflictions, the moral discipline of taming through deportment, the moral discipline of meditative concentration and the uncontaminated moral discipline; and so make the lineage of the Three Jewels flourish."

Then Aniruddha said this to the Blessed One: "If the Tathāgata will bless me through the might of his miraculous power, I shall ask the Tathāgata a little." He asked: "What is it that is called the Three Jewels? If the Three Jewels called the Buddha, the Dharma and the Saṅgha have no distinct characteristics, why are there distinctions and differences between the Dharma and the Saṅgha, and how is taking refuge in the Three Jewels to be held? If the three refuges were one, it would not be right to speak of three. If three refuges are taught, why are seven kinds of assembly taught? Blessed One, to what does one go for refuge, that it is called taking refuge?"

Then the Blessed One explained those points one by one, and declared this: "'Buddha' means awareness, because he is directly aware of all the characteristics of dharmas. Furthermore, all sentient beings in the three realms are continually drunk with sleep, but the Buddha has opened the eye of awakening; since he himself is aware and also makes others aware, he is called awareness. The Buddha, having obtained the means for all dharmas, has taught everything."

Aniruddha asked, "How is it that the Buddha is called one who has taught everything? Is it said of his teaching according to whatever suits the faculties without missing the right time, or is it said of his teaching in accordance with the distinct divisions?"

The Buddha declared, "The Buddha taught all dharmas according to the faculties, without missing the right time; later, when the disciples compiled the Dharma piṭaka, they set it out in its several divisions. On some occasions the Buddha laid down for the disciples the moral disciplines, heavy and light, what is a transgression and what is not, and so the Vinaya Piṭaka was distinguished. On some occasions, because afflictions and secondary afflictions arise from the characteristics of cause and result, he taught the characteristics of karma, and so the Abhidharma Piṭaka was distinguished. What he taught and explained as suited to the world with its gods and humans is called the Collection Increasing by One (Ekottarāgama); it was taught as the practice of persons who have the fortune to be disciplined. The many kinds of profound meaning he taught to sentient beings of sharp faculties are called the Middle Collection (Madhyamāgama); it was taught as the practice of the disciples. The various kinds of dharmas of meditative concentration he taught are called the Miscellaneous Collection (Saṃyuktāgama); it was taught as the practice of persons who practice meditative concentration. In order to refute the many non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), he taught the Long Collection (Dīrghāgama)."

It was asked: "If the Buddha abides in having spoken everything, then what of this? In a sūtra, the Buddha was seated at the foot of a tree, and taking hold of the leaves of one branch of that tree, he said to his attendants, 'Are the leaves of this branch I hold many, or are the leaves of the great tree many?' They replied, 'The leaves of the tree are many.' The Buddha in turn said, 'The Dharma that I know is like the leaves of the tree; the Dharma that I have taught is like the leaves held in the hand.' Since he said this, may it be said that the Buddha has spoken everything?"

He declared: "There is the 'all' that is all things of distinct characteristics, and the 'all' that is all things of a single characteristic. Here, with the 'all' of distinct characteristics in mind, it is explained that the Buddha has spoken everything. Moreover, that is said with regard to sentient beings being unable to grasp it exhaustively; it is not that the Buddha is unable to speak it. Furthermore, it is with respect to his knowing everything that he is said to have spoken everything; it is not permissible to say that he has spoken everything as a means of guiding beings."

It was asked: "If the Buddha speaks by means of his knowing, and śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas also speak on the basis of knowledge, why should they not also be given the name buddha?"

He declared: "It is not so. The Buddha has reached the culmination of both knowing and speaking, whereas the knowledge and speech of the two vehicles have not reached the culmination of the Dharma. Furthermore, because the Buddha knows all dharmas thoroughly, he also knows the names that are designated for them; the two vehicles are not able to do so. Furthermore, the dharmas the Buddha has attained are limitless and the Dharma he teaches is limitless; the two vehicles are not able to do so. Furthermore, as for what is shared in what the Buddha has attained, some of it is shared with what the two vehicles attain, and some is not shared with what the śrāvakas attain. The knowledge shared by the three vehicles is attained by the middle vehicle. The knowledge shared by the two vehicles is attained by the Buddha alone, and what the two vehicles do not know is known by the Buddha alone. Furthermore, the larger the box, the larger its lid: when the Dharma is great, its characteristics too are limitless. Therefore the Buddha, with limitless wisdom, teaches limitless Dharma, whereas the wisdom of the two vehicles is limited, so it is not designated the characteristics of the Dharma. Furthermore, he possesses both faculty and object. Faculty means the faculty of wisdom. Object means wisdom. The object of the faculty means the dharmas that wisdom apprehends. The Buddha is complete in both faculty and object, and there is no dharma apprehended by wisdom that he has not fathomed to the end. The two vehicles are not complete in both faculty and object. Furthermore, the Buddha has attained the true wisdom that knows the characteristics of all dharmas correctly, just as they are. The two vehicles do not know the depths of the Dharma thoroughly, so their knowledge may not be called true wisdom. Thus, for many kinds of reasons, the two vehicles may not be called buddhas.

"The name Blessed One may not be applied on the basis of words and sounds; it is applied on the basis of meaning, and so he is called the Blessed One, because he knows all the remedial dharmas. Furthermore, since the sounds and languages of the world do not agree, worldly people themselves simply do not understand them, but the Buddha, knowing them all, is called the Blessed One. Furthermore, in the text of the one called the monk Pārśva it is said that, among those of the two vehicles and ordinary beings, some have faith in solitude and peace, some have faith in meditative concentration and meditative absorption, and some, because of other conditions, keep it secret and do not teach it; but the Dharma that the Buddha has attained is endowed with the power of loving-kindness and compassion, so he delights in showing and speaking it to others. In another sense, he is called the Blessed One because he has conquered the three poisons."

It was asked: "If those of the two vehicles have also conquered the three poisons, why are they not designated Blessed Ones?"

He declared: "It is not so, because those of the two vehicles are subject to falling back, whereas the Buddha is not subject to falling back. Falling back is of three kinds: falling back from a fruit, falling back that is not from a fruit, and falling back in the activity of practice.

"Falling back from a fruit means that the three fruits of the śrāvakas are subject to falling back, while the final fruit does not fall back. The middle vehicle is of two kinds. If one is a pratyekabuddha who has practiced yoga for as long as a hundred eons, the fruit does not fall back. If one has become a pratyekabuddha from the three fruits of the lesser vehicle from the outset, that fruit is subject to falling back. But the fruit of a buddha does not fall back.

"Falling back that is not from a fruit is when a person headed toward the three vehicles turns back without having attained the fruit, and when a monk who practices yoga is lazy, lacks diligence, and declines in the practice of yoga and does not exert himself. These three are called falling back that is not from a fruit.

"Falling back in the activity of practice means being unable to engage directly, as an activity, in all the dharmas that are to be attained. For example, with the ten powers of a buddha or the ten knowledges of the lesser vehicle, one engages in one of them and the rest are not needed; or when someone who knows a hundred thousand verses of the sūtras by heart is at a time when he is not reciting them, that is called falling back in the activity of practice with respect to all of them.

"The lesser vehicle too has non-falling back from a fruit, and the middle vehicle too has falling back that is not from a fruit, but the Buddha has no falling back that is not from a fruit, because there is no dharma in which he has not exerted diligence. Those of the two vehicles have falling back from practice, but for the Buddha it is not fixed one way: since engaging in one of the ten powers suffices and the other nine need not be engaged, it is called falling back. Or, in another sense, he has no falling back, since he does not need to engage in activity. For example, if a person knows two hundred thousand verses of the sūtras by heart, relying on his ability he can recite them completely in one or two days, but the Buddha completes them in a mere instant. The ten powers are just the same: if they need to be engaged, he accomplishes immeasurable deeds in a mere instant, so he has no falling back on account of not needing to engage in activity."

"Furthermore, the Buddha has no falling back on account of not needing to engage in activity. For example, when he puts on his lower garment, he does not put it on without any rule, as ordinary beings do. Moreover, whatever enlightened activity he performs is for the benefit of many sentient beings: if there is benefit, he performs that action; if there is no benefit, he does not perform it. Thus he has no falling back on account of not needing to engage in activity; each act has its own purpose, and since this is not fixed in one way, it is called the inconceivable intention of the Buddha."

It was asked: "Why is it that, for the śrāvakas, the three fruits are subject to falling back, while the final fruit is not?"

He declared: "The three fruits are subject to falling back because they were attained before. The final fruit is not subject to falling back because it had never yet been attained. For example, just as a poor person who comes upon delicious food does not forget it for a long time, so it is with this point. Furthermore, the final fruit becomes the unobstructed path of patience and becomes the path of the wisdom of liberation. The three fruits are subject to falling back because they have not become the unobstructed path of wisdom and have not become the path of the wisdom of liberation. Furthermore, the path of seeing the truths does not fall back, but the path of contemplation falls back, because it cuts the knots of the afflictions by means of both pure and impure perceptions. In contemplation there is defilement, but in the seeing of the truths there is no defilement: through seeing the truths there is no defilement, and through seeing the truths one also sees the defilements; and through seeing the meaning, contemplation of the profound meaning also arises, so there is no falling back into defilement. Furthermore, the power of the wisdom that sees the truths is great, like a great tree trunk pressing down on goods, whereas the wisdom of contemplation and mental attention is weak, so there is falling back. Furthermore, in seeing the truths, the two minds of patience and wisdom of the desire realm cut the knots of the ninefold defilement, and the two minds of patience and wisdom of the higher realms cut the seventy-two defilements. Having reached the culmination of the formless realm, there is no falling back. For that reason, he alone is called the Blessed One.

"Furthermore, the Buddha has no trace of habitual tendencies, but those of both vehicles have not exhausted their habitual tendencies. For example, the monk called Ever Chewing always chews the cud like an ox, because in former lives he was an ox for many lifetimes and was then born as a human. For example, a certain monk, though his defilements were exhausted, always looked into a mirror, because for many lifetimes he had been a woman who sold herself and was then born as a human. For example, a certain monk delighted in climbing onto seats and racks, because for many lifetimes he had been a monkey and was then born as a human. Such as these may not be called Blessed Ones."

It was asked: "As for the words 'Thus have I heard,' was it said 'I heard' while the Buddha was still living, or was it said 'heard' after the Buddha had passed into nirvāṇa? If in the words of the compilation of the treasury of the Dharma it says 'I heard,' did he not say, 'Please teach and explain to me, without omission, what the Buddha has taught'?"

It was asked: "How was whatever was spoken as words of Dharma during those twenty years taught to him?"

He declared: "Through the Buddha's skillful means, he shows countless dharmas even with a single verse, and he shows the meaning of countless dharmas with a single verse. Even if the Buddha taught only a brief outline, Ānanda knew it without omission, because the power of his retention, through his sharp wisdom, was great.

"As for the saying that there are eighty thousand dharmas: just as, for example, the roots, branches, twigs, and leaves of a tree are collectively called one tree, so the Dharma that the Buddha taught for the sake of sentient beings from beginning to end is called one collection; in that way there are eighty thousand."

Again: "If the Buddha, without moving from a single seat, taught the Dharma for twenty years, and Ānanda did not hear it, why does he say 'I heard'?"

He declared: "It may also be said that the gods told Ānanda. Or it may also be said that the Buddha, the Blessed One, through his conventional, worldly mind, caused Ānanda to know it. Or it may also be said that it was the Dharma spoken to the monks. Furthermore, Ānanda made this request to the Buddha: 'Please do not give me the Buddha's old robes, and do not give me the alms of the patrons. I serve the Buddha and attend on him, providing whatever he wishes, because I desire the Dharma, not for the sake of clothing and food. The other monks see the Buddha at the times of morning and afternoon; please let me see him whenever I wish to look.' Furthermore, the Dharma was spoken for twenty years, and the teaching of the Dharma is called one collection; in that way there are eighty thousand.

"Or again, sixteen syllables are half a verse, and thirty-two syllables are called one verse; in that way there are eighty thousand. Or again, from the long and short lines of verse, forty-two syllables make one verse; in that way there are eighty thousand. Or again, the teaching of moral discipline each half month is called one collection; in that way there are eighty thousand. Or again, sixty-six thousand verses spoken by the Buddha are called one collection; in that way there are eighty thousand. Or again, because the Buddha taught eighty thousand medicines of the Dharma as antidotes to eighty thousand afflictions, they are called the eighty thousand doors of the Dharma."

It was asked: "Why did the Buddha not mention the sūtras and the Abhidharma first, but mention the Vinaya first?"

He declared: "Because it is supreme, and because it was laid down by the Buddha. For example, in the sūtras there are also teachings of the Dharma given by disciples. For example, there is also the case where Śakra, lord of the gods, said, 'I am supreme among those who give gifts. Why is that? Because by giving gifts I became lord of the gods, and my wishes are fulfilled as I desire,' and the Buddha too said, 'It is so.' At certain times the Buddha emanated emanated buddhas, and there are also teachings of the Dharma spoken by emanated buddhas. But the Vinaya is not like that: all of it was spoken by the Buddha, and therefore the Buddha mentioned it first."

"Or again, the sūtras were spoken according to whatever place he was staying in, but the Vinaya is not like that. If an offense occurred in some place, the rule was not laid down right there but was laid down outside the boundary. If an offense occurred among householders, it was laid down within the Saṅgha; if an offense occurred in a town, it was likewise laid down within the Saṅgha; and if an offense occurred among the five kinds of assembly, it had to be laid down among the monks and nuns. Therefore the Buddha mentioned it first.

"As for 'Vaiśālī': some lands are named after the name of a king, some are named after the lie of the land, and some are named after a city. This land was given its name from a nāga. As for 'the place of Kalandaka': it was named after a bird, and 'city' is the name of the lord of the city. As for 'Devadatta': because his parents prayed to the gods and obtained a son, he was given the name Devadatta, 'Given by the Gods.'

"As for 'mighty and rich': riches are of two kinds, the sentient kind and the non-sentient kind. The non-sentient kind means having much gold and silver, treasuries of the seven kinds of precious things, wealth, fields, and houses. The sentient kind means having many male servants, female servants, laborers, oxen, horses, cattle, and sheep, and many villages and households under one's control. That is called rich. 'Mighty' means having power, or having excellent qualities, so that all people show him respect and hold him in honor; therefore he is called mighty. 'Turning to the Three Jewels, he took the three refuges.'"

It was asked: "What is the nature of the three refuges?"

"Some teachers say that the three refuges are a ritual act, and that a ritual act has no defining characteristic. When one takes the three refuges, kneeling, joining the palms, and uttering the three refuges aloud in words is the manifest ritual of body and speech. If one has made the mind one-pointed, then although body and speech are present, there is nothing manifested by the ritual; that is called the unmanifest ritual. Otherwise, the three refuges are said to be the nature of the three actions of body, speech, and mind. Or again, the three refuges are the five virtuous aggregates: through the five virtuous aggregates of a sentient being, one goes for refuge to the three, and the Three Jewels are the place in which one takes refuge.

"'Going for refuge' has the meaning of protection and refuge, or of an ally and support. For example, a man who has committed a great offense flees to another country and seeks refuge there with the foreign king, and the king of that country commands him, 'If you wish to be free of fear, do not leave this country of mine, and heed what I teach; then I will protect you.' Ordinary beings are just the same: since they have fallen under the power of Māra and suffer the misfortune of circling in birth and death, they should turn to the Three Jewels and go for refuge. If one turns one-pointedly to the Three Jewels and goes for refuge, and does not act contrary to the Buddha's teaching, Māra, the evil king, can do nothing at all.

"Once, a pigeon threatened by a hawk went into Śāriputra's shadow, yet it was still frightened and terrified. Then, when it went into the Buddha's shadow, it was without fear and did not tremble at all. Even if the great ocean might shift and change, that pigeon could not be moved. This is because the Buddha possesses great loving-kindness and great compassion, while Śāriputra does not have great loving-kindness and compassion. The Buddha has no habitual tendencies, while Śāriputra has not exhausted his habitual tendencies. The Buddha practiced the conduct of a bodhisattva for three countless eons, while Śāriputra practiced austerities for sixty eons. Therefore, when the pigeon went into Śāriputra's shadow it was still frightened and afraid, but when it went into the Buddha's shadow it became fearless."

It was asked: "If by going for refuge to the Three Jewels evil and nonvirtue are purified and one has no cause for fear, why did one such as Devadatta, who went for refuge to the Three Jewels, went forth from home out of faith, and was even complete in moral discipline, commit three deeds of immediate retribution and fall into the Avīci hell?"

He declared: "Protection, too, is given as refuge to those who are fit to be given refuge. One such as Devadatta committed great nonvirtuous evil and was bound up with karma that must certainly be experienced, and for that reason it is hard to protect him."

It was asked: "If the Buddha does not protect those who have committed great evil, what need is there for the Buddha to protect those who have no evil karma? And how can it be said that the Three Jewels have the power of protection?"

He declared: "Devadatta's going for refuge to the Three Jewels was not done with a genuine mind, so his three refuges were not complete. Because he constantly desired gain, honor, and fame, he even set himself up against the Buddha, saying, 'I have the wisdom of omniscience.' Therefore, although the Three Jewels have great power, they did not become a protection for him. Consider, for example, one such as King Ajātaśatru. He had the evil of a deed of immediate retribution and deserved to fall into the Avīci hell, but because he asked the Buddha for refuge with a genuine, one-pointed mind, the evil of immediate retribution was purified. He fell into the Black Line hell (Kālasūtra), and just as one among humans experiences heavy evil, he experienced something like that weight for only seven days, and by that all his evil was purified. That is called the power of the protection of the Three Jewels."

It was asked: "It is said that Devadatta, having committed evil, cannot be protected. What then is the meaning of what is said in the sūtras, 'Whoever goes for refuge to the Buddha will not fall into the three lower realms'?"

He declared: "Because Devadatta went for refuge to the Three Jewels, although he went to the Avīci hell, the suffering he experiences is lighter and smaller, and there are intervals of rest."

"For example, when a person has gone to a mountain, a forest, or a wilderness, a place of fear, if he brings the Buddha to mind and thinks of him, then by the Buddha's qualities his fear and terror will be calmed. Therefore it is no lie that, if one goes for refuge to the Three Jewels, they become one's protection and refuge."

It was asked: "Under which of the four truths are those called the Three Jewels included? Under which of the twenty-two faculties are they included? Under which of the eighteen elements are they included? Under which of the twelve sense sources are they included? Under which of the five aggregates are they to be included?"

He declared: "Among the four truths, the Three Jewels are included under the truth of cessation, and they are one part of the truth of the path. As to the faculties, they are included under the faculty of coming to know what was not known. Among the eighteen elements, they are included under the mind element, the mind-consciousness element, and the element of mental phenomena. Among the twelve sense sources, they are included within the mind sense source and the sense source of mental phenomena. Among the five aggregates, they are included within the five undefiled aggregates.

"Now, among the four truths, the Buddha Jewel is one part of the truth of the path. Among the four truths, the Dharma Jewel is included under the truth of cessation. Among the four truths, the Saṅgha Jewel too is one part of the truth of the path. Among the twenty-two faculties, the Buddha Jewel is included under the faculty of final knowledge. The Dharma Jewel, being the truth of cessation, is unconditioned and so is not included under any faculty. Among the twenty-two faculties, the Saṅgha Jewel is included within the three undefiled faculties.

"Among the eighteen elements, the Buddha Jewel is included within one part of the mind element, the mind-consciousness element, and the element of mental phenomena. Among the twelve sense sources, it is included within one part of the mind sense source and the sense source of mental phenomena. Among the five aggregates of appropriation, it is included within one part of the five undefiled aggregates.

"Among the eighteen elements, the Dharma Jewel is included within one part of the element of mental phenomena. Among the twelve sense sources, it is included within one part of the sense source of mental phenomena.
