The Dharma Jewel is not included in the five aggregates, because the five aggregates are conditioned, whereas the Dharma is unconditioned.

"Among the eighteen elements, the Saṅgha Jewel is included within one part of the mind element, the mind-consciousness element, and the element of mental phenomena. Among the twelve sense sources, it is included within one part of the mind sense source and the sense source of mental phenomena. Among the five aggregates, it is included within one part of the five undefiled aggregates."

It was asked: "When one says, 'I go for refuge to the Buddha,' does one go for refuge to the Buddha Śākyamuni, or does one go for refuge to the buddhas of the three times?"

He declared: "One goes for refuge to the buddhas of the three times. Why is that? Because the Dharma body of the buddhas is one and the same. Going for refuge to one buddha is itself going for refuge to all buddhas, because there is no difference among the buddhas.

"Or again, as for saying, 'I go for refuge to the buddhas of the three times': among the gods, some say, 'I am a disciple of the Buddha Kāśyapa,' and some say, 'I am a disciple of the Buddha Vipaśyin.' In the same way, with regard to each of the seven buddhas, each of them says, 'I am the disciple of such-and-such a buddha.' Therefore it is held, 'It is fitting that I go for refuge to one buddha; the three times are not appropriate.' But there are also those who say that this is not right. Why is that? For example, in the Sūtra Asked by Vaiśravaṇa it is said that King Vaiśravaṇa, in going for refuge to the Three Jewels, went for refuge to the buddhas of the past, to those yet to come, and to those now present. If one connects it with that meaning, it is evident that one goes for refuge to the buddhas of the three times."

It was asked: "If that is so, what is the meaning of the sons of the gods each saying, 'I am the disciple of such-and-such a buddha'?"

He declared: "What definitive settling of the meaning is there in what the sons of the gods say? Some sons of the gods go for refuge to one buddha, and some go for refuge to the buddhas of the three times. In short, it is done with regard to one buddha."

It was asked: "Going for refuge to what is called going for refuge to the Buddha?"

He declared: "'Going for refuge' is a wheel of speech. It is going for refuge to the qualities of omniscience and of one who has nothing more to learn."

It was asked: "Does one go for refuge to the form body, or does one go for refuge to the Dharma body?"

He declared: "One goes for refuge to the Dharma body; one does not go for refuge to the form body, because form is not the Buddha."

It was asked: "If the form body is not the Buddha, why does drawing blood from the Buddha's body become a deed of immediate retribution?"

He declared: "The form body is the vessel of the Dharma body; it is the abode of the Dharma body. Therefore doing harm to the form body becomes a deed of immediate retribution; it is not that it becomes a deed of immediate retribution because the form body is the Buddha."

It was asked: "Going for refuge to what is called going for refuge to the Dharma?"

He declared: "'Going for refuge' is a wheel of speech. Going for refuge to nirvāṇa, which has reached the end of both desire and freedom from desire, is called going for refuge to the Dharma."

It was asked: "Does one go for refuge to the ending of one's own body, or does one go for refuge to the ending of the bodies of others?"

He declared: "Going for refuge to the ending of one's own body and to the ending of the bodies of others is called going for refuge to the Dharma."

It was asked: "How does one go for refuge to the Saṅgha?"

He declared: "Going for refuge is a wheel of speech. Going for refuge to the qualities of the śrāvakas, both those still in training and those beyond training, who are a field of merit, is called going for refuge to the Saṅgha."

It was asked: "Does one go for refuge to the Saṅgha of conventional truth, or does one go for refuge to the Saṅgha of ultimate truth? If one goes for refuge to the Saṅgha of ultimate truth, then when the Buddha long ago conferred the three refuges on Trapuṣa and Bhallika, it would not have been fitting for him to say, 'Later there will be what is called the Saṅgha; go for refuge to it,' since the Saṅgha of ultimate truth is always present in the world."

He declared: "Because the Saṅgha of conventional truth is the basis on which the ultimate Saṅgha depends, he said, 'Later there will be a Saṅgha; go for refuge to it.' Or again, he said this so that the Saṅgha of conventional truth would be respected and honored. For the Buddha, among all assemblies, the Saṅgha is the supreme assembly of the Buddha. For example, curd comes from milk, butter comes from curd, and the essence of butter comes from butter, and the essence of butter is the supreme and most excellent. The Saṅgha, the Buddha's assembly, is just like that. When there is a Saṅgha of disciples, there are within it those who have entered the four paths and attained the four fruits, an unsurpassed field of merit. Therefore, among the ninety-six kinds of assembly, the Saṅgha is the most excellent, supreme, and without equal. Therefore there is no contradiction with the meaning in his saying, 'Later there will be a Saṅgha; go for refuge to it.'"

It was asked: "The Buddha himself is the Dharma, the Dharma itself is also the Saṅgha, and the Saṅgha itself is also the Dharma, so in reality they are a single dharma. What distinction or difference is there?"

He declared: "Although those dharmas are so, there are differences in terms of meaning. As for what are called the Three Jewels: one who, without being taught, has great wisdom and has attained the ground of no more training, and who possesses all qualities, is called the Buddha Jewel. The truth of cessation, the unconditioned, is called the Dharma Jewel. Those who possess the qualities and wisdom of the śrāvakas in training and beyond training are called the Saṅgha Jewel.

"Divided in terms of dharmas: the dharmas that are untaught and beyond training are called the Buddha Jewel. The truth of cessation, the unconditioned, the dharma that is neither in training nor beyond training, is called the Dharma Jewel. The śrāvakas in training and beyond training are called the Saṅgha Jewel.

"Divided in terms of the faculties: the Buddha is the faculty of final knowledge. The Dharma is not a dharma of the faculties. The Saṅgha is the three undefiled faculties.

"Divided in terms of the truths: the Buddha is one part of the truth of the path. The Dharma is the truth of cessation. The Saṅgha too is one part of the truth of the path.

"Divided in terms of the fruit of the ascetic (śramaṇa): the Buddha is a śramaṇa. The Dharma is the fruit of the śramaṇa. The Saṅgha is śramaṇas. The Dharma Jewel is the fruit of the śramaṇa.

"Divided in terms of the brahmin: the Buddha is a brahmin. The Dharma is the fruit of the brahmin. The Saṅgha is brahmins. The Dharma Jewel is the fruit of the brahmin.

"Divided in terms of pure conduct: the Buddha is pure conduct. The Dharma is the fruit of pure conduct. The Saṅgha is pure conduct. The Dharma Jewel is the fruit of pure conduct.

"Divided in terms of cause and result: the Buddha is a cause. The Dharma is the result. The Saṅgha is a cause. The Dharma Jewel is the result.

"Divided in terms of path and result: the Buddha is the path. The Dharma is the result. The Saṅgha is the path. The Dharma Jewel is the result.

"The teacher of the Buddha is the Dharma. Since the Buddha is born from the Dharma, the Dharma is the mother of the Buddha, and the Buddha abides in the Dharma."

It was asked: "If the teacher of the Buddha is the Dharma, why is the Dharma not mentioned first among the Three Jewels?"

He declared: "Although it is the Dharma that is the Buddha's teacher, the Dharma is not taught by anyone other than the Buddha, and what is called the path must be accomplished by a person. Therefore the Buddha is mentioned first."

Then Aniruddha again said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, when one takes refuge in the Three Jewels, if one mentions the Dharma Jewel first and the Buddha Jewel afterward, is it valid as going for refuge to the three?"

He declared: "If one has no understanding or knowledge, then even if the order is mistaken there is no fault, and it counts as going for refuge. If one knowingly and deliberately says it wrongly, there is a fault, and it does not count as going for refuge to the three."

It was asked: "If one mentions the Buddha and the Dharma but does not mention the Saṅgha, is it valid as the three refuges? If one mentions the Dharma and the Saṅgha but does not mention the Buddha, is it valid as going for refuge? If one mentions the Buddha and the Saṅgha but does not mention the Dharma, does it count as going for refuge to the three?"

He declared: "None of these is valid; they do not count."

It was asked: "Without going for refuge to the three, may one obtain the five precepts? Without going for refuge to the three, may one obtain the eight-branch precepts? Without going for refuge to the three, may one obtain the ten precepts? Without performing the formal act of a motion and three proclamations (jñapticaturthakarman), may one receive the full precepts?"

He declared: "None of these is valid. If one is to receive the five precepts, one should first go for refuge to the three. After going for refuge to the three, one comes to obtain the five precepts. Then one should state what the five precepts are, so that the other person knows the names of the five precepts. In the same way, it is by performing the formal act of a motion and three proclamations that one comes to the full precepts, and after that, the four supports, the four root offenses, and the thirteen saṅghāvaśeṣas should be stated and taught so that they are known and understood.

"Or again, if, after the going for refuge, the precept of not killing is taught, one comes to obtain the precepts. Therefore it is said that by explaining one precept one obtains the five precepts: if one can keep one precept, it is said that one can keep all five. Or again, it is because the five precepts are connected with one another, and because from the outset one has vowed to receive the five precepts. Or again, the precepts are obtained after the five precepts have been received. Some teachers say that the statement 'having performed the three refuges, one obtains the five precepts' is true in meaning, just as with the performance of the formal act of motion and proclamation."

Taking the eight-branch precepts, taking the ten precepts, and taking the five precepts are as has been explained.

He asked, "If someone has earlier gone for refuge to the Three and become a layman, and later takes the five precepts, or else the ten precepts, must he then go for refuge anew?"

He declared, "Even without performing the three refuges again, because he earlier went for refuge to the Three, he obtains all the precepts."

He asked, "If one has gone for refuge to the Three but has not performed the formal act of motion and proclamation, does one obtain the precepts?"

He declared, "Those who take the five precepts, the eight-branch precepts, and the ten precepts obtain the precepts by going for refuge to the Three. But in taking moral discipline in full, moral discipline becomes complete through performing the formal act of a motion and proclamations, four in all, and not through going for refuge to the Three. Because the qualities and merit of taking moral discipline in full are great, it cannot be accomplished by a mere slight act or a mere slight condition. Therefore one obtains that moral discipline through the three refuges and through performing the fourfold act of motion and proclamation within a Saṅgha of ten. The five precepts, the eight-branch precepts, and the ten precepts are not so great in qualities and power, and therefore one obtains that moral discipline by going for refuge to the Three; many acts and many conditions are not needed."

He asked, "After someone has taken moral discipline in full, only the four root offenses and the thirteen saṅghāvaśeṣas are taught, while the others are not taught in full. Why is that?"

He declared, "These two kinds of moral discipline are the weighty ones within. If even one of the four roots is broken, it cannot be restored. A saṅghāvaśeṣa can indeed be restored, but it is hard to restore: one must undergo the probation that satisfies the Saṅgha and the act of penalty, and then confess the offense before a Saṅgha of twenty. Because these are hard to guard, they are mentioned so that they will be guarded. The others are easy to guard if one trains in them, so there is no need to mention them. That is why these two kinds are mentioned and the others are not."

He asked, "Is this prātimokṣa discipline an undefiled discipline, or is it the discipline of meditative concentration?"

He declared, "It is neither the undefiled discipline nor the discipline of meditative concentration. As for this prātimokṣa discipline, if a buddha has come into the world, this discipline exists; if a buddha has not come into the world, this discipline does not exist. The undefiled discipline and the discipline of meditative concentration exist at all times, whether or not a buddha has come into the world. The prātimokṣa discipline comes into being through being laid down, but the undefiled discipline and the discipline of meditative concentration do not come into being through being laid down. The prātimokṣa discipline is obtained from others, but the undefiled discipline and the discipline of meditative concentration are not obtained from others. The prātimokṣa discipline is present at all times, whether one is asleep or awake, and whether the mind is virtuous, nonvirtuous, or indeterminate. The undefiled discipline and the discipline of meditative concentration are present while one is sitting in meditative concentration and when one is settled in undefiled mind, but they are absent in all other states of mind. The prātimokṣa discipline exists only among humans, but the undefiled discipline and the discipline of meditative concentration exist among both gods and humans. The prātimokṣa discipline exists only in the desire realm, but the undefiled discipline and the discipline of meditative concentration exist fully in the desire realm and the form realm. The undefiled discipline and the prātimokṣa discipline exist only among the disciples of the Buddha, but the discipline of meditative concentration exists among non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) as well."

He asked, "Of the five precepts of a layman, how many concern wrongs by nature, and how many concern wrongs by prescription?"

He declared, "Four concern wrongs by nature; the one precept on drinking alcohol concerns a wrong by prescription. Drinking alcohol is a ground for heedlessness and laziness, and it becomes an accomplice to the four wrongs. Among the five precepts, drinking alcohol is the basis of heedlessness, and so it also causes the other four precepts to be broken. For example, in the time of the Buddha Kāśyapa, a certain layman, on account of drinking alcohol, lusted after another man's wife and committed sexual misconduct with her; he also stole his neighbor's chicken and killed it. When someone asked him, 'Why do you act like this?' he said, 'I did no such thing.' Thus, through drinking alcohol, he broke all four precepts at once. If one drinks alcohol, one may even commit the four acts of immediate retribution; only causing a schism in the Saṅgha one cannot do. And although this is not brought about by past karma, one will nonetheless experience madness, a madness without mindfulness. Through drinking alcohol one becomes intoxicated like a maddened bull elephant and turns quarrelsome. When drunk on alcohol, one lets slip the proper activities one should engage in: meditative concentration, reading the sūtras, recitation, and the formal acts of the Saṅgha. So although it is not a wrong by nature, it is like a wrong by nature."

Then Aniruddha again said to the Blessed One, "Are precepts such as the precepts of a layman obtained only with respect to sentient beings, or may they also be obtained with respect to what is not a sentient being? Are the precepts obtained with respect to sentient beings who can be killed, from whom one can steal, with whom unchaste conduct can be engaged in, and to whom lies can be told? Or are the precepts also obtained with respect to sentient beings who cannot be killed, from whom one cannot steal, with whom unchaste conduct cannot be engaged in, and to whom lies cannot be told?"

He declared, "With respect to sentient beings, four precepts are obtained; with respect to what is not a sentient being, the precept of not drinking alcohol is obtained."

With respect to everything that counts as a sentient being, whether it can be killed or cannot be killed, whether unchaste conduct can be engaged in with it or cannot, whether one can steal from it or cannot, whether lies can be told to it or cannot, from all of them moral discipline is obtained. From the Avīci hell upward and from the sphere of neither perception nor nonperception downward, all that has life within the great trichiliocosm world-system, up to and including the Tathāgata, are those from whom these four kinds of moral discipline may be obtained. For at the outset, when taking moral discipline, one vows, "I shall not take the life of any living being, I shall not take anything that is not given, I shall not engage in unchaste conduct with anyone, and I shall not tell any lie," and no limit or measure is set to this. Therefore there is no sentient being from whom moral discipline is not obtained.

When the conferral of moral discipline is performed according to the rite, first, so that the candidates will understand and have faith, the Dharma is taught, and they are made to give rise to a mind of loving-kindness and compassion toward all sentient beings. When such a mind of superior aspiration arises in this way, a superior moral discipline is obtained.

Further, in obtaining moral discipline, the four kinds of moral discipline are obtained separately from each and every sentient being, and if each of the four is divided, they become twelve kinds of moral discipline. The four faults that are reversed by not killing sentient beings, not taking what is not given, not engaging in unchaste conduct, and not telling lies arise on account of three causes and conditions: they arise from attachment, they arise from aversion, and they arise from delusion. With respect to every sentient being there are these twelve nonvirtues, and if one turns away from these nonvirtues, one obtains twelve manifest forms of virtuous moral discipline (vijñaptirūpa). It is the same for what is obtained from all the immeasurable sentient beings.

Even if a hundred times ten thousand or a hundred times a hundred thousand arhats pass into nirvāṇa, if one first obtained moral discipline from those arhats, then even though the arhats have passed into nirvāṇa, the moral discipline is not impaired.

As for the precept of not drinking alcohol, from the beginning of this body to its end, one obtains a manifest form of the precept with respect to every single draught of all that exists throughout the great trichiliocosm world-system. Since at the outset, when taking the precept, one has vowed, "I shall not drink any alcohol," even if all the alcohol were used up, the moral discipline would always remain and would not be impaired.

He asked, "If at the outset, when taking the precepts, one took the precept of not engaging in sexual misconduct with any woman through the three doors, and later one takes a wife, is this moral discipline broken?"

He declared, "It is not broken. Why is that? At the outset one obtained the precept of not engaging in sexual misconduct with women; but since this is one's own wife, it is not sexual misconduct, and so the precept is not broken. When this principle is applied, everything is consistent: the obtaining of moral discipline in the eight precepts and the ten precepts, with respect to sentient beings and to what is not a sentient being, is likewise the same.

"As for the two hundred and fifty precepts, here too one obtains seven precepts separately with respect to each and every sentient being, and when these are divided they become twenty-one precepts. With respect to every sentient being seven wrongs of body and speech can occur, and these wrongs arise through three kinds of causes and conditions: they arise through attachment, they arise through aversion, and they arise through delusion. Thus the seven wrongs arise through three causes and conditions, and so the seven nonvirtuous wrongs times three make twenty-one. If one turns away from those wrongs, that becomes moral discipline; and just as there are twenty-one manifest forms of moral discipline with respect to a single sentient being, so it is with respect to all sentient beings. When this principle is applied, countless kinds of moral discipline can be obtained at one time, yet they are not all broken at once at one time, nor do they all cease at one time.

"As for what is called broken moral discipline: if the root of moral discipline is broken, one cannot advance from one level to a higher one, and even if one gives back the moral discipline and later takes it again, one does not obtain moral discipline. For example, if the root of the one-day eight-branch precepts is broken, one may not later take any of these kinds of moral discipline: the eight precepts, the five precepts, the ten precepts, the full precepts, the discipline of meditative concentration, or the undefiled discipline. If the root of the five precepts is broken, then even if one takes the eight precepts, the ten, the full moral discipline, the discipline of meditative concentration, or the undefiled discipline, none of them can be obtained. If, after the five precepts have been broken, one gives back the five precepts and takes the ten precepts, that is not possible. If, after giving back the five precepts, one takes the five precepts again, or takes the eight precepts, the ten precepts, the full precepts, the discipline of meditative concentration, or the undefiled discipline, one obtains no precept at all. If the root of the ten precepts or of the full precepts is broken, then advancing from one level to a higher one, or giving back the precepts and taking precepts again, is just as was explained for the five precepts."

He asked, "Of these three kinds of moral discipline, the discipline of meditative concentration, the undefiled discipline, and the prātimokṣa discipline, which is called the supreme discipline?"

He declared, "The discipline of meditative concentration is supreme; yet in another respect the prātimokṣa discipline is also supreme. How is that? This discipline exists only if a buddha comes into the world, whereas the discipline of meditative concentration and the undefiled discipline exist at all times. The prātimokṣa discipline can be obtained with respect to both sentient beings and what is not a sentient being, whereas the discipline of meditative concentration and the undefiled discipline are not obtained with respect to all sentient beings. The prātimokṣa discipline is obtained through a mind of loving-kindness toward all sentient beings, whereas the discipline of meditative concentration and the undefiled discipline are not obtained through a mind of loving-kindness."

"The prātimokṣa discipline is what keeps the seven kinds of assembly that uphold the Buddha's Dharma present in the world, and it becomes the basis on which the fruits of the three vehicles continue unbroken. The discipline of meditative concentration and the undefiled discipline are not like this, and therefore it is the highest and most excellent in the three realms.

"At the outset, when one takes the precepts, moral discipline becomes complete the instant the formal act of a motion and proclamations, four in all, has been performed. The manifest form of moral discipline in the very first instant of mind is called both 'action' and 'path of action.' The manifest form of the precepts that arises in the later instants of mind is called 'action,' but it is not a 'path of action.' Thus, from the first instant of mind, the intention of the manifest form of moral discipline is fully complete, and because it is endowed with intention it is called the path of action of intention. With the earlier moral discipline acting as the cause, the manifest form of the remaining moral discipline comes about spontaneously, and therefore it is called 'action,' but not 'path of action.'

"The precept of the single first instant of mind is accompanied by the rite, and it is not without the rite either. The precepts that arise successively afterward possess only what is without the rite; they are not accompanied by the rite. The precept of the single first instant of mind is called moral discipline, it is also called virtuous conduct, and it is also called Vinaya. The moral discipline that arises successively, immediately after it, also has that same meaning."

Then Aniruddha again asked the Blessed One, "Of the three times taken together, in which time is moral discipline obtained?"

He declared, "The precepts are obtained by a single instant of mind in the present time. The past and the future are dharmas, and because they are not sentient beings, they do not obtain the precepts; but the single present instant of mind is a sentient being, and so it obtains the precepts."

He asked, "Is moral discipline obtained by a virtuous mind, by a nonvirtuous mind, or by an indeterminate mind? Or are the precepts obtained by one who is without mind?"

He declared, "They are obtained by all of these. At the outset, with a virtuous mind, one bows at the feet of the Saṅgha, takes up the robes and the alms bowl, and makes the request to whoever is the preceptor. Then, squatting with palms joined, one completes the precepts with a virtuous mind, without interruption, through the formal act of a motion and proclamations, four in all; this is called obtaining moral discipline by a virtuous mind.

"Suppose that while the rite is being performed in this way, step by step, one has been continually endowed with a virtuous mind, but then, at the time when the officiant performs the fourfold act of motion and proclamation, unvirtuous thoughts such as attachment or aversion arise. Even though that same being comes to possess the manifest form of moral discipline, this is called obtaining the precepts by a nonvirtuous mind. Yet in this case the precepts are obtained by the force of the earlier virtuous mind and the virtuous act; they are not obtained by the nonvirtuous mind.

"Suppose one has first performed the acts of the rite with a virtuous mind, and then, at the time the fourfold act of motion and proclamation is performed, one falls asleep or dozes off, and the manifest form of the precepts arises from that sleeping and drowsy mind; this is called obtaining the precepts by an indeterminate mind.

"Suppose one has first performed the rite with a virtuous mind, and then, at the time the fourfold act of motion and proclamation is performed, one enters into equipoise in the samādhi of cessation. Since at that time one comes to possess the manifest form of the precepts, this is called obtaining the precepts while without mind."

Aniruddha again asked, "If a householder, without having taken the five precepts, directly takes the ten precepts, does he obtain the precepts?"

He declared, "He obtains both kinds at one time, the precepts of a layman and the precepts of a novice (śrāmaṇera). If, without having taken either the five precepts or the ten precepts, one directly takes the full precepts, one obtains all three kinds of precepts at one time."

Aniruddha asked, "Blessed One, if by taking the full precepts one obtains all three kinds of precepts at one time, why must one proceed in stages, first taking the five precepts, then after that taking the ten precepts, and only later taking the full precepts?"

He declared, "Although one does obtain all three kinds of moral discipline at one time, whoever has strong devotion to the Buddha's Dharma will surely proceed in stages. First one takes the five precepts and is tamed, and once faith and devotion have grown strong, one then takes the ten precepts. Having taken the ten precepts, once the roots of virtue have grown strong, one then takes the full moral discipline. In this way, step by step, one gains the taste of the Buddha's Dharma, and one's devotion becomes firm and indestructible. For example, just as entering the great ocean becomes deeper only gradually, so too is entering the ocean of the Buddha's Dharma. If one takes the full moral discipline all at once, the proper sequence is lost and one's deportment also suffers.

"Moreover, regarding moral discipline, there are some sentient beings who attain the fruit of the path by taking the five precepts, and there are some sentient beings who attain the fruit of the path on the basis of having taken the ten precepts. Because causes and conditions differ in these ways, the Tathāgata taught a proper sequence.

"If one first takes the five precepts and then takes the ten precepts, then at the time of taking the ten precepts one possesses both sets of precepts. If after taking the five precepts and the ten precepts one takes the full precepts, then at the time of taking them in full one possesses all three sets of precepts.

"Among the seven kinds who take the five precepts, the ten precepts, and the full precepts, only the precepts conferred through the fourfold act of motion and proclamation are obtained in stages over three times. The remaining six kinds of taking are obtained at a single time; for them there is no sequence of three times.

"Although the three kinds of moral discipline are thus obtained at a single time, they are distinct. When one gives back moral discipline and says, 'From today onward I am a novice, not a monk,' then although the full moral discipline no longer exists, the other two kinds of moral discipline do not cease to exist; they remain. If, having taken the five precepts and the ten precepts, one says, 'From now on I am a layman, not a novice,' then only the ten precepts cease to exist, while the five precepts remain."

"If one says, 'I give back all the precepts of a householder and of one gone forth; accept me as a layman who has gone for refuge,' then although the three kinds of moral discipline cease all at once, the three refuges do not cease. The obtaining of the three kinds of moral discipline in sequence, the rite of giving them back, and the order of these, are just as was taught for obtaining the precepts at a single time."

He asked, "If someone who was formerly a layman goes forth and takes the ten precepts, do the five precepts not cease to exist?"

He declared, "They do not cease to exist. Only the name and the rank are exchanged; the precepts do not cease to exist. That is, one loses the name of layman and gains the name of novice; one loses the rank of a householder and gains the rank of one gone forth."

He asked, "If one takes the full moral discipline of a monk, do the ten precepts and the five precepts not cease to exist?"

He declared, "They do not cease to exist. Here too, only the name and the rank are lost: one loses the name of novice and gains the name of monk; one loses the rank of novice and gains the rank of monk. From beginning to end it is moral discipline; it is only the name that is applied for the time being. It is like the leaves of a cultivated tree: in spring and summer they are green, in autumn yellow, and in winter gray. Changed by the seasons, the leaves of the tree take on different colors, yet from beginning to end they are nothing but the leaves of the tree. Moral discipline is likewise: it is always just one moral discipline, but it becomes different through time. Again, for example, milk, curd, butter, and ghee are distinct as four kinds, yet though they differ in time they are like one and the same thing. Moral discipline is likewise: though it differs through the three times, there is no difference in the moral discipline itself."

Aniruddha again asked, "Blessed One, if a layman taking the precepts is not able, by his capacity, to take them in full, and takes from one or two up to four precepts, does that count as having taken moral discipline?"

He declared, "He does not obtain the precepts by that."

He asked, "If he does not obtain the precepts, then what is the meaning of the statements in the sūtras that there are laymen who practice a small part, laymen who practice a large part, and laymen who practice the whole?"

He declared, "That was said with reference to the greater and lesser qualities that come from guarding the precepts. It does not mean that moral discipline may be taken in that way."

Aniruddha again asked, "Blessed One, if someone takes the five precepts for one day, or for two days, up to ten days, taking them in that way, does he obtain the precepts?"

He declared, "That is not permissible. The moral discipline laid down by the Buddha has, in each case, a fixed time and measure. One who takes the five precepts must without fail take them for as long as he lives. One who takes the eight-branch precepts must without fail take them for one day and night. Therefore what you first proposed is not permissible.

"Taking the precepts by performing the fourfold act of motion and proclamation also has superior, middling, and inferior levels. Of these, the five precepts are the inferior precepts, the ten precepts are the middling precepts, and the full precepts are called the superior precepts. The five precepts too have three levels, superior, middling, and inferior. If one takes the precepts with a small and feeble mind, they are called inferior precepts. One who takes moral discipline with a middling mind obtains middling precepts. If one takes moral discipline with a superior mind, one obtains superior precepts. That the full moral discipline also has three levels, superior, middling, and inferior, is just as was taught for the five precepts.

"If, after first taking the precepts with an inferior mind, one later takes the ten precepts with a superior or middling mind, the five precepts do not grow or become superior beyond what they were before. But apart from the five precepts, the remaining five precepts, up to not eating food at the wrong time, become superior precepts, while the five precepts taken earlier remain inferior. If one first takes the five precepts with an inferior mind and afterward takes the full precepts with a superior or middling mind, here too the five precepts taken earlier do not grow or become superior. But apart from the earlier five precepts, all the other precepts, because they were taken with a superior and exceptional mind at the time of taking the full moral discipline, become superior and exceptional moral discipline.

"Therefore the prātimokṣa discipline is not obtained in a twofold ranking. If it is divided by sequence, the five precepts are called inferior, the ten precepts are called middling, and the full moral discipline is called superior. If it is divided according to meaning, then when one takes the five precepts with a superior mind, those precepts are called superior; when one takes the ten precepts with a middling mind, they are called middling precepts; and when one takes the full moral discipline with an inferior mind, it is called inferior moral discipline. Applied to the meaning, the obtaining of moral discipline from a superior, middling, or inferior mind does not correspond to the sequence, and there is no single fixed determination.

"If someone has requested a preceptor to confer the ten precepts, then even if the preceptor is not present at the time the ten precepts are taken, the ten precepts are obtained. If the preceptor dies at the time the ten precepts are taken, and one knows or has heard that he has died, then even though one takes the precepts one does not obtain them. If one does not know or has not heard that he has died, one obtains the precepts. In taking moral discipline through the fourfold act of motion and proclamation, if the preceptor is not present, then even though one takes the precepts one does not obtain them, because the number of the Saṅgha is not complete. If the number of the Saṅgha is complete, the precepts may be taken even without the preceptor."

Aniruddha again asked, "Blessed One, may a layman who holds the five precepts engage in trade for profit?"

He declared, "He may engage in trade for profit, but he should not engage in these five kinds of business. He should not engage in the business of trading in livestock that goes on foot; if he has livestock of his own, he is allowed to sell it, but he may not sell it to a butcher. He may not engage in trading bows, arrows, and weapons for profit, though he may sell what he himself owns. He may not engage in the business of trading in alcohol for profit, though he may sell what he himself owns. He may not press sesame oil, because many insects die. That is so according to the custom of India; on this side of Kapiśa, if there are no insects in the oil being pressed, there is no fault.

"Dyeing and boiling with the five kinds of great colored dyes is also not permitted, because many insects die. In other countries, such as Yogsha, many insects die from the dyeing and boiling of colors, and therefore it is not allowed. In the land of China too, the boiling of indigo-blue dye kills many insects, and so it is included among the five kinds of dyeing and boiling."

Aniruddha again asked, "Blessed One, if, besides not eating food at the wrong time, which is the fast, what is called the eight precepts of a single day would make nine, why is it called eight?"

He declared, "What is called a precept is, by its nature, the fast of not eating food at the wrong time. It is a branch of the eight precepts; because it serves as their support, they are called the eight-branch precepts, and not nine."

He asked, "If someone takes the eight-branch precepts, in which of the seven kinds of assembly is that person included?"

He declared, "Even though he has not taken the precepts for life, he has the precepts of a single day, and so he too is called a layman. Then again, he cannot be called a layman, since he has not taken moral discipline for life; and he cannot be called not a layman, since he has the precepts of a single day. Therefore the sūtras also say that he is an intermediate person."

Aniruddha again asked, "Blessed One, is there any prātimokṣa discipline apart from that of the seven assemblies?"

He declared, "There is: it is precisely the eight-branch precepts. In accordance with that meaning, one who has taken the eight-branch precepts is not included among the seven kinds of assembly. Since, by the rule for taking the eight-branch precepts, the words are fixed as 'I shall not take life for one day and night,' they may not be mixed with the other precepts that are taken for life."

He asked, "May one take the eight-branch precepts all at once for two days, or three, up to ten days?"

He declared, "From the very beginning the Buddha laid them down as precepts for a single day and night, and so one may not go beyond that. If one is able, by one's capacity, to take them, one must take them one day and night at a time, each time anew; in that way it is permissible to take them for many days. The method of taking the eight-branch precepts for a single day and night is that one must take them from another person. And from whom must one take them? One must take them from any one of the five assemblies.

"If, after taking the eight-branch precepts, one beats or abuses a sentient being, the moral discipline is not pure. Even if on the day one took the precepts one did not beat or abuse anyone, but resolved to beat or abuse someone the next day, the moral discipline is still not pure. In short, however much improper action one does with body and speech, although the precepts are not broken, they are not pure. Even if body and speech are pure, if the mind gives rise to conceptual thoughts of attachment, or to conceptual thoughts of harm driven by aversion, they are again not pure. Even if the three actions of body, speech, and mind are pure, if one does not bring the six recollections to mind, that too is not called pure moral discipline. If, after taking the eight-branch precepts, one brings the six recollections to mind, it is called pure moral discipline.

"The sūtras say that the merit of a king of Jambudvīpa, who holds sway over all the people within the realm of Jambudvīpa and over all the gold, silver, and wealth found there, does not amount to even a sixteenth part of the merit of the pure eight-branch moral discipline.

"Whoever has resolved to take the eight-branch precepts but carelessly indulges in music at will, or carelessly eats delicious food, or carelessly enjoys all sorts of amusements as he pleases, and then afterward takes the precepts, whether in the morning or in the afternoon, does not obtain the precepts. If, having had no intention beforehand of taking the precepts, one has carelessly done all sorts of things, and afterward meets a spiritual friend and takes the precepts, one obtains the precepts at any time, whether in the morning or in the afternoon. If one set out to take the precepts but an obstructor or an obstacle arose, so that one had no control over it, and later, once there is no obstructor or obstacle, one takes the precepts, one obtains them at any time, whether in the morning or in the afternoon."

He asked, "If one takes precepts that one will keep by day but not keep by night, does one obtain the eight-branch precepts? And if one takes precepts that one will keep by night but not keep by day, does one obtain the eight-branch precepts?"

He declared, "In neither case are the precepts obtained. For from the very beginning the Buddha laid them down as precepts of a single day and night; this is a fixed measure, and it may not be contravened."

Aniruddha again asked, "Blessed One, if the precepts are not obtained in that way, then what of the account in the sūtras that the monk Koṭikarṇa saw, in a remote wilderness, some hungry ghosts undergoing all sorts of suffering, some of whom enjoyed pleasures by day and suffered by night, and some of whom enjoyed pleasures by night and underwent suffering by day? And what of the conflicting idea that, in their former lives among humans, these had kept the precepts by day and done evil by night, or else kept the precepts by night and done evil deeds by day?"

He declared, "One cannot rely solely on the causes and conditions of former lives. Moreover, this account is not taught in the sūtras or in the Vinaya, so it cannot be called the definitive true meaning. Elsewhere it is said that what was described there was an emanation produced by the noble Kātyāyana in order to tame Koṭikarṇa, and was not real.

"When someone who has taken the eight-branch precepts gives the precepts back, it is not necessary to give them back to the five kinds of assembly; by saying a single sentence to one other person, 'I give back the precepts,' the precepts are given back.

"If one distinguishes in terms of who obtains the prātimokṣa precepts, then of those belonging to the five classes of destiny, only those of the human class obtain the precepts; those of the remaining classes do not.

For example, those belonging to the class of gods do not obtain the precepts, because they are attached to and fixated on the enjoyment of pleasures. For instance, once an attendant of the noble Maudgalyāyana was struck by illness, and the noble Maudgalyāyana went up to the heaven of the Thirty-Three to consult the physician Jīvaka. At that time the sons of the gods were just about to go and play in the pleasure grove of Supreme Delight, and the noble Maudgalyāyana was sitting in the middle of the road along which they were going; yet the sons of the gods went past without the leisure even to glance at the noble Maudgalyāyana. When Jīvaka came up level with him, he merely raised his hand to Maudgalyāyana from a distance and drove on with his chariot.

Then the noble Maudgalyāyana thought, "When this Jīvaka lived among humans in the past, he was my disciple. Now he is attached to the enjoyments of the gods, and his former memory has declined." Having thought this, by his miraculous power he blessed Jīvaka's chariot so that it could not move. Then Jīvaka got down from the chariot and bowed at the feet of the noble Maudgalyāyana, and Maudgalyāyana told him of many and various causes and conditions.

Jīvaka said to Maudgalyāyana, "Because I was the venerable one's disciple when I lived among humans, I at least raised my hand and greeted you. Do any of the other sons of the gods do even that much? Those born as gods are fixated on and attached to the enjoyment of pleasures, and so, having no control over themselves, they become like that."

Maudgalyāyana said to Jīvaka, "A disciple has been struck by illness and is sick. How should he be treated?"

Jīvaka said, "For the time being he should not eat food."

Then Maudgalyāyana urged Śakra (Indra), lord of the gods, with these words: "Since it is extremely rare to meet a buddha who has come into the world, why do you not go to him from time to time and discuss the sublime Dharma?"

Then Śakra, lord of the gods, knowing with his mind the thought of the noble Maudgalyāyana, commissioned a son of the gods and sent him to summon another son of the gods; but though he was called as many as three times, he did not come. That son of the gods had no more than a single goddess and a single musician, yet because he was attached to and fixated on desire, he could not bear to leave them, strict though the command of the lord of the gods was, and only came later. Then, when Śakra asked him why this had happened, he told him honestly how it had come about.

Śakra said to Maudgalyāyana, "This son of the gods has no more than a single goddess and a single musician, and yet he cannot bear to give them up. As for my own sport and delight: I have become lord of the gods, surrounded by many and various celestial palaces and by countless goddesses, enjoying and sporting with all kinds of divine food of a hundred flavors and hundreds of thousands of divine musicians, so that when I look to the east I forget even the west. How much more, then, must this be so for me! Though I know that meeting a buddha who has come into the world is difficult, and that hearing the sublime Dharma is extremely difficult, I am bound and caught by the pleasures of desire and have no control over myself, so that even though I know it, I am unable to act on it."

In the practice of taking moral discipline, the precepts are obtained by vowing resolutely with a heroic and courageous mind. In someone like a son of the gods, attached as he is to the pleasures of desire, the unwholesome mind predominates; and if the power of the wholesome mind is weak, by what force could he obtain the precepts? Those like the hungry ghosts do not, because they suffer from hunger and thirst, and their bodies and minds blaze and burn. Hell beings are tormented by countless sufferings of heat and cold, they experience all kinds of unbearable things, and their bodies and minds are in anguish, so they have no cause for obtaining the precepts. Those like the animals are obscured by karma and lack understanding, so for them there is no rite of taking the precepts. Where some sūtras say that on certain occasions nāgas took moral discipline, this means that, taking the eight-branch precepts with a wholesome mind, they obtained merit through that wholesome mind for the space of one day and night; but because they are obscured by karma, they did not obtain moral discipline.

If one distinguishes in terms of the four continents, the humans living in southern Jambudvīpa, in western Godānīya, in eastern Videha, and on the small islands in the ocean that belong to these three continents obtain the precepts. For example, the Buddha prophesied that in western Godānīya noble ones would dwell in assembly, and at present, since the great work of the Buddha is spreading there, the four kinds of assembly are also present. In eastern Videha too there are monks, and since the Buddha's Dharma is practiced there as well, the four assemblies are present. Only in northern Kuru is there no Buddha's Dharma, and moral discipline is not obtained there. That is because they are obscured by the ripening of meritorious karma and obscured by delusion, and so they have not become vessels for the Dharma of the noble ones.

Persons are of four kinds. What are they? Male, female, the paṇḍaka (one of neither sex), and one with both sexual characteristics. Of these four kinds, males and females can obtain the precepts; paṇḍakas and those with both characteristics do not obtain the precepts. Moreover, among males and females, whoever has killed father or mother, killed an arhat, drawn blood from the body of a buddha with malicious intent, caused a schism in the Saṅgha, engaged in unchaste conduct with a nun, robbed travellers on a narrow pass, lived in the community by theft, or severed the roots of virtue: none of these persons obtains moral discipline. But if one examines this in terms of the great purpose, one who has deep faith and devotion toward the Buddha's Dharma is not included here. Whether a god, a nāga, a hungry ghost, a yakṣa, one of northern Kuru, a paṇḍaka, one with both sexual characteristics, or even one who has committed all kinds of evil and unwholesome deeds, such a one may take the three refuges.

It was asked, "Are the buddhas of the three times equal in their obtaining of moral discipline?"

He declared, "They are not equal. What is called obtaining moral discipline is that the precepts are obtained with respect to the realms of sentient beings and of what is not a sentient being. When a single buddha comes into the world, he acts for the benefit of countless sentient beings and leads them to nirvāṇa without remainder; therefore the buddhas who come into the world afterward do not obtain moral discipline with respect to any of those beings. Thus the buddhas, earlier and later, are not equal in their obtaining of moral discipline. For example, the Buddha Kāśyapa tamed countless sentient beings and led them to nirvāṇa without remainder; and whereas the Buddha Kāśyapa obtained moral discipline with respect to all of those beings, the Buddha Śākyamuni did not obtain moral discipline with respect to any of them."

"All buddhas are alike, equal in these three things. What are they? They are equal in the accumulations of their conduct, equal in the Dharma body, and equal in acting for the benefit of sentient beings. All buddhas alike practiced the conduct of a bodhisattva for three countless eons, perfected the Dharma body of five parts, and perfected the ten powers, the four fearlessnesses, and the eighteen unshared qualities of a buddha; and all of them led countless sentient beings into nirvāṇa."

It was asked, "Since the sūtras say that when a single buddha comes into the world he acts so that some ninety koṭis of niyutas of sentient beings pass into nirvāṇa, why do you say that he acts for the benefit of countless sentient beings?"

He declared, "When the sūtras say that a single buddha who comes into the world acts for the benefit of some ninety koṭis of niyutas of sentient beings, this refers to those who are liberated by that buddha. Among the sentient beings who are liberated, there are some whose benefit the Buddha himself accomplishes, some whose benefit his disciples accomplish, and some who are liberated by the Dharma he leaves behind. The words 'ninety koṭis of niyutas of sentient beings' refer to those whose benefit the Buddha accomplishes himself; the words 'he led countless sentient beings into nirvāṇa without remainder' refer to the benefit he accomplishes in general. All the buddhas of the three times are equal in those respects, but in their obtaining of moral discipline they are not equal."

It was asked, "Is what is called the discipline of non-restraint obtained with respect to sentient beings and also what is not a sentient being? Is that discipline obtained with respect to everything, both what is appropriated by consciousness and what is not?"

He declared, "The discipline of non-restraint is obtained with respect to sentient beings; the discipline of non-restraint is not obtained with respect to what is not a sentient being. According to another view, the discipline of non-restraint is obtained with respect to sentient beings that may be killed, but that discipline is not obtained with respect to sentient beings that may not be killed. According to yet another view, the discipline of non-restraint is said to be obtained with respect to both, sentient beings that may be killed and those that may not. For example, a butcher who slaughters sheep is always equipped with the intention to kill and attends only to slaughtering sheep, so there is no measuring its extent. Even if he is born among gods or humans, and does not at once commit the act of killing, still, as he circles from one birth to the next, there is the possibility that he will be reborn again as a sheep. Therefore the discipline of non-restraint is obtained with respect to all sentient beings, and the twelve disciplines of non-restraint are likewise.

"The twelve disciplines of non-restraint are these: the butcher, the executioner, the pig breeder, the poultry breeder, the fisherman, the hunter, the bird catcher, the snake catcher, the one who casts spells on nāgas, the jailer, the thief, and the catcher of thieves: these are the twelve. Raising silkworms is also included among the disciplines of non-restraint.

"There are three occasions on which the discipline of non-restraint is lost: at the time of death, when attachment and fixation are exhausted, and when one takes the moral discipline of the vows. For example, when one takes the three refuges, by saying the first phrase at the beginning, the discipline of non-restraint is lost; then, when one says the second and the third phrases, the moral discipline of virtue is obtained."

It was asked, "When a person does the work of non-restraint, at what moment is the moral discipline of virtue lost and the discipline of non-virtue obtained?"

He declared, "When one first says the single phrase 'I am a butcher,' the discipline of virtue is lost, and by saying 'I am a butcher' a second and a third time, the discipline of non-virtue is obtained. According to another view, the discipline of non-virtue is obtained at the very moment the discipline of virtue is given up. If a person who keeps the discipline of virtue has not made the commitment 'I am a butcher,' but, attached merely to profit and gain, raises piglets and does the work of slaughter, then at that time his discipline of virtue is said to be lost; this is called giving up the discipline of virtue and obtaining the discipline of non-virtue. If he definitely commits himself, saying 'I am a butcher,' then too the discipline of non-virtue is obtained. The discipline of non-virtue is obtained merely by one's own commitment; it need not be received from anyone else. If one commits to take the discipline of non-virtue for one day and night, or two, up to ten days and nights, or for one year or two years, it is obtained accordingly, for as long or as short a time as one committed to. Therefore evil, non-virtuous things make one sink into the cycle of birth and death; they have no power to raise one higher and higher. Thus the discipline of non-virtue is obtained through the very deed itself, and is not like the moral discipline of virtue."

This was the eighth chapter, called Aniruddha, from The Great Skillful Means by Which the Buddha Repays Kindness.

The Great Skillful Means by Which the Buddha Repays Kindness. Seventh scroll, the last.

Moreover, when bodhisattva great beings, who know kindness, repay kindness, and with great skillful means act for the benefit and happiness of sentient beings, know the right time and occasion, their teaching and correcting do no harm.

Son of good family, a buddha appeared in the world called Fearless King, a tathāgata, an arhat, a perfectly complete buddha, endowed with knowledge and conduct, a sugata, knower of the world, unsurpassed guide of beings to be tamed, the Teacher, the Victor. He came into the world and acted for the benefit of the fortunate. Having acted for the benefit of the fortunate, he passed into parinirvāṇa.

After he had passed into nirvāṇa, while the sublime Dharma was being practiced, there arose a brahmin's son, upright in mind and possessed of sharp wisdom, who kept the five constant precepts and upheld the sublime Dharma. At a later time this brahmin's son set out for another country on business. Since the road that travellers took was very dangerous, he went in the company of five hundred fellow travellers. When he and those companions reached the dangerous place, there were five hundred bandits living in that dangerous place, who constantly attacked the travellers passing through.

At that time, intending to attack those travellers, the bandit chief sent a man ahead to watch and observe how the travellers were going. Now among the bandits there was a man who had once been a close friend of the brahmin's son, and he came ahead among those travellers and warned the brahmin's son, saying, "Son of good family, understand this: tonight, in the first part of the night, enemies will come. Whatever happens, find some way to escape, so that you yourself are not harmed or wounded."

When the brahmin's son heard these words, he became as uneasy as a man who has something stuck in his throat that he can neither swallow nor spit out. He thought, "If I speak of this to my companions, I fear my companions will kill that man; and if my companions kill that man, my companions will experience countless sufferings in the three lower realms. If I do not tell my companions, the enemies will kill my companions; and if they kill my companions, the enemies will fall into the three lower realms and experience countless sufferings." Having thought this, he thought further, "I shall act as a means to the benefit and happiness of sentient beings. Even if I myself fall into the three lower realms and experience suffering, that is easy to bear." Having thought this, he took up a sword, killed the man who was to carry back the report, and so brought his companions to safety.

Then, when his companions saw what had happened, they said to the brahmin's son, "Son of a brahmin, you are the best among men and supremely virtuous. Why have you now committed such a great evil here?"

Then the brahmin's son set his knee on the ground, joined his palms, and with a mind full of wonder said to his companions, "Today I have committed a great evil that ought not to be done; and I did it in order to benefit sentient beings and to bring my companions to safety."

His companions said, "You killed a man; how can you say that you brought us to safety?"

The brahmin's son said, "This man I have killed was a cruel enemy. This enemy was coming to kill you, my companions; so, for your sake, my companions, I cut off this man's life, so that you may reach your country safely. As for me, even if I experience the sufferings of the hell beings, that is easy to bear."

Then the five hundred companions cried aloud and wept, and said, "Under the whole sky there is nothing so precious as life; of all terrors none surpasses death. Therefore all sentient beings, for the sake of body and life, will give up gold, silver, jewels, lands, wives, children, even food and clothing. Today we have gained back our lives once more." Having said this, they continued, "Son of a brahmin, because you disregarded the sufferings of the three lower realms for the sake of sentient beings, we remember your very great kindness. Remembering this great kindness, today we raise the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening." So saying, all of them raised the mind set on awakening.

Then the five hundred bandits also said, "You are said to be the best of all and supremely virtuous; why have you committed such a great evil?"

The brahmin's son said, "I have committed a great evil that ought not to be done; and I did it in order to benefit all sentient beings and to protect your bodies and lives."

Then the bandits said to the brahmin's son, "You yourself killed a man; what benefit is there in that for us?"

The brahmin's son said, "Although I knew from the start that you bandits were here, I did not speak of it. I did not report it to the king, nor did I speak of it to my companions. Because I did not tell of it, your bodies and lives have remained safe."

When the bandits heard these words, they thought, "Only now are our bodies and lives truly spared." Having thought this, they joined their palms toward the brahmin's son and said, "Great being, excellent, excellent! You who are endowed with great compassion, please teach us what we should do from this day on."

The brahmin's son said, "Quickly raise the mind set on unsurpassed awakening."

Then the bandits too, in order to repay his kindness, at once raised the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening.

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "Bodhisattvas who abide in diligence, who adorn awakening and wish to repay the kindness of the buddhas, should always regard all sentient beings as an only child, and keep them in mind. Son of good family, understand this: do not think that the brahmin's son at that time, on that occasion, was someone else. Do not regard it so. The one who was then the brahmin's son is I myself now. Through that cause and that condition I passed beyond some ninety eons of saṃsāra and swiftly became fully and perfectly awakened in unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening."

Moreover, this is how a bodhisattva who practices great skillful means knows kindness and repays kindness. Once, when the Buddha, the Blessed One, was staying in the Bamboo Grove, a certain monk was struck by a disease of sores on his body. Pus and blood flowed continually from all over his body, so that no one wanted him, and he was put in a ruined, wretched hut outside the monastery. At that time the Tathāgata, by the power of his miraculous ability, blessed things so that not one of his large retinue knew of it, and the Tathāgata went alone to the sick monk, intending to wash him with water. Then, to bring good fortune to the gods of the desire realm, Śakra, lord of the gods, surrounded by a retinue of hundreds of thousands of countless gods, let fall divine flowers like rain, played divine music of every kind, and stationed himself in the sky above.

At that time the lord of the gods of the heaven of the Thirty-Three filled an excellent vase, formed from a hundred merits, with the pure water of great compassion, and holding it in his hand he bowed his head at the feet of the Tathāgata, came forward to meet the Tathāgata, and stood to one side. Then the Tathāgata stretched out his hand, endowed with a hundred merits, and from his five soft, long fingers he sent forth great rays of light, illuminating the gods and that whole great assembly. While that great assembly stayed where it was, the Tathāgata himself went up to the sick monk, sent forth rays of light from the crown of his head, and illuminated the sick monk. The instant the light touched the sick monk, the suffering of his illness was eased. With his body still covered in the pus and blood of his sores he begged for refuge, but he could not bow to the Tathāgata with his body. So the Tathāgata took the jeweled vase from Śakra, lord of the gods, with his right hand and poured the water over the crown of the sick monk's head, and with his left hand he washed and anointed the sores on the sick monk's body; and wherever the Tathāgata's hand passed, all the sick monk's sores were healed.

When all his sores were healed in this way, the monk was overjoyed, delighted and glad, and he said, "Homage to the Buddha Śākyamuni! Homage to the father of great compassion! Homage to the supreme, unsurpassed king of physicians! Today the illnesses of my body have been healed, but the illness of my mind alone is not yet healed. I beg the Tathāgata, out of his loving concern, to grant me the medicine of the Dharma and to heal the illness of my mind as well."

Then the Tathāgata spoke these words to the sick monk: "Because the Tathāgata regards your kindness as very great, the Tathāgata has repaid your kindness."

At that the sick monk became exceedingly joyful. The Tathāgata inspired him, gladdened him and encouraged him, and the sick monk attained the fruit of an arhat and came to possess the three knowledges, the six superknowledges and the eight liberations.

Śakra, lord of the gods, and all the divine assembly were in doubt, and they said to the Tathāgata, "Why did the Tathāgata himself do this cleansing, washing away all the blood from the sores on the sick monk's body, and say that this monk has shown him very great kindness? Please explain and expound the meaning of this to us."

The Buddha spoke these words to Śakra, lord of the gods, and to the great assembly of gods: "Listen well and keep it well in mind, and I shall explain these matters. Śakra, lord of the gods, long ago in the past, countless, measureless eons ago, there was an evil king who acted against the Dharma and was given to plundering everyone. At that time that evil king summoned five hundred ministers, made a secret plan with them, and binding them to secrecy, ordered those ministers as follows: 'Whoever breaks the law, I empower you to punish him. Whatever wealth you obtain, you are to share it with me.' At that time those five hundred constantly carried out floggings; those from whom they got much wealth they let go accordingly, while those who had no wealth they harmed, even to the point of losing their lives.

"At that time a certain layman committed a small breach of the law, and he was led before the five hundred to be flogged. But because they had heard that this layman was very virtuous, they released him without doing him harm, and on that account the layman too was overjoyed.

"Kauśika, understand this: the five hundred at that time, on that occasion, were this sick monk. The one who at that time, on that occasion, was the layman is I myself now. Therefore a bodhisattva repays even a light kindness from countless eons before with a great one, and never forgets it, right up until he is fully awakened."

At that Śakra, lord of the gods, and the great assembly of gods became exceedingly joyful, and some eighty-four thousand of the divine assembly raised the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. Having raised the mind set on awakening, they played divine music and went back each to his own abode.

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "A son of good family or a daughter of good family who knows kindness and repays kindness should devote himself to these four things. What are the four? Relying on a spiritual friend, listening to the Dharma with a one-pointed mind, attending to the meaning, and practicing just as one has been taught.

"Moreover, one should rely on four things. What are the four? Rely on the Dharma, not on the person; rely on the meaning, not on the letter; rely on wisdom, not on consciousness; rely on the sūtras of definitive meaning, not on the sūtras of provisional meaning. One who devotes himself to these eight things is called one who knows kindness.

"Moreover, one who possesses eight things is called one who repays kindness. What are the eight? They are not to heed gain, profit, disparagement, praise, fame, ridicule, suffering or happiness.

"Moreover, one who devotes himself to these four things is called one who knows kindness, and is also called one who repays kindness. What are the four? They are these: through having cultivated loving-kindness, when one sees evil sentient beings, one gives rise to a mind of love and tender affection; through having cultivated compassion, when one sees suffering sentient beings, one looks on them without weariness and does not abandon them; through having cultivated joy, when one sees teachers, masters, parents and those endowed with good qualities, one gives rise to a joyful mind; and through having cultivated equanimity, when one sees sentient beings who have become one's enemies, one does not give rise to a mind of anger."

Then Ānanda arranged his robe and spoke these words to the Buddha:

"Blessed One, when the Tathāgata first raised the mind set on awakening, how did he devote himself to the four things that have to do with knowing kindness and repaying kindness?"

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "Long ago in the past, countless, immeasurable, measureless eons ago, there appeared in the world a buddha called Vipaśyin, a tathāgata, an arhat, a perfectly complete buddha, endowed with knowledge and conduct, a sugata, knower of the world, unsurpassed guide of beings to be tamed, the Teacher, the Victor, and he acted for the benefit of the fortunate. Having acted for the benefit of the fortunate, he passed into parinirvāṇa. When the sublime Dharma, and the practice of its semblance as well, had died out, in the land called Vārāṇasī the people were happy and at ease, the harvests were good, and the king governed the country according to the upright Dharma.

"In that land there was a mountain called Noble Sage Mountain, and on that mountain five hundred pratyekabuddhas always dwelt. Besides them, many sages endowed with the five superknowledges also dwelt there. At that time many wild animals and beasts of prey lived on that mountain as well. Among them was a lion called Firm Vow, the fur of whose body was the color of gold. He was of great might and splendor; when he roared, even the birds fell to the ground and the wild animals fled. As he roamed about that mountain, he met a pratyekabuddha who had the appearance and bearing of a renunciant, and a mind of great joy toward him arose in the lion. Day after day he stayed close to him and continually heard him reciting sūtras and teaching the sublime Dharma.

"At that time a great and cruel hunter saw the lion with its golden pelt, and a mind of great delight arose in him. He thought, 'If I kill that golden lion, skin it and present the pelt to the king, I shall certainly obtain a great reward, and for seven generations my family will be free of want.' Having thought this, he thought further, 'This lion called Firm Vow is the king of the beasts of prey, so he cannot be brought down even with bow and arrow, and nothing can be done with snares and nets either. Now I shall use a stratagem like this: since this lion, king of beasts, follows after renunciants, I too shall take on the appearance of a renunciant. I shall hide my bow and arrows under a Dharma robe, lower my eyes, walk with slow steps and sit at the foot of a tree. As soon as he sees me, he will surely come up to me; and if he comes up to me, I shall draw my bow to the full, and if I shoot him with a poisoned arrow, he will surely die.'

"Having thought this, he went back home and said, 'My family has lived by hunting for generations, yet I have never even heard of a beast of prey with golden fur, let alone seen one. Now, in order to kill it, I shall shave my hair and beard.' Then he put on the Dharma robe, went back once again to that mountain, and sat at the foot of a tree.

"Then the lion Firm Vow saw the monk and was overjoyed. Bounding and leaping, he came up close to the monk and licked his feet. Then the hunter shot an arrow at the lion. Stricken with pain by the poisoned arrow, the lion roared and was about to seize him and spring, when he thought, 'This man is wearing the dyed robe of a renunciant, the noble banner of the buddhas of the three times. Though it would not be hard for me to harm him, if I harm him and separate him from his life faculty, I shall have defiled the noble banner of the buddhas.' Having thought this, he stayed where he was and did nothing.

"Before long the poison took effect, and the pain became all the more unbearable. Again he thought, 'Shall I devour him and tear him apart?' But then he thought, 'That would not be hard; yet if I harm him, the buddhas and the noble ones will censure me, saying that in the world there is no difference between those who do good and those who do evil. This man is evil, for he came to kill me; but if I do not show patience toward him, I shall be no different from that evildoer. Whoever cultivates patience, everyone will come to love; whoever is impatient, everyone will come to hate, and afflictions will arise in him. When afflictions have arisen, he circles in the saṃsāra of birth and death. Circling in birth and death, he is born in states without leisure. Born in states without leisure, he is parted from spiritual friends. Parted from spiritual friends, he does not hear the sublime Dharma. Not hearing the sublime Dharma, he is caught in the net of doubt. Caught in the net of doubt, he is parted from unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. Therefore I shall not give rise to an evil mind.' Having thought this, he spoke these verses:

'Better that my body and life should perish
Than that I give rise to an unwholesome, evil mind.
Even though I should be parted from my life,
I shall not harm one who wears the saffron robe.
Toward those who have gone forth
I shall never give rise to an evil thought.'

"Having spoken these verses, he died. Heaven and earth shook six times. The beasts of prey and wild animals fled in terror in the four directions. Rain fell without clouds, and even the sun lost its light.

"Then the hunter took off the robe, took up a knife, skinned the lion and went back home. Having gone back home, he presented the pelt to the king. When the king saw the pelt he was overjoyed, and he said to his ministers, 'Since I was born I have never heard that there was any animal whose pelt had fur the color of gold. Now to see it here directly with my own eyes is a great wonder, a great wonder!' And he asked the hunter, 'By what means did you obtain such a pelt?'

"Then the hunter said to the king, 'Great king, if you grant me immunity, I will speak truthfully.' The king commanded, 'It is granted to you, just as you wish,' and the hunter told him just what had happened.

"When the king heard these words, he was overwhelmed with grief, and like a man with something stuck in his throat that he can neither swallow nor spit out, he was ill at ease. He gathered together all the great ministers, the vassal kings and the whole great assembly, and commanded as follows:

"All of you, understand this. Long ago I heard learned and wise men say, 'If, among those born in the animal realm, one has body hair the color of gold, it is without doubt a bodhisattva.' If even a single ordinary person gives rise to the mind set on unsurpassed awakening, he accomplishes the great aim of benefit and happiness for all sentient beings. Why did this evil hunter use such a means and kill a bodhisattva? If I were to give him a minister's post, or a reward, or a Nepalese elephant, or a horse, or the seven precious things, or clothing, or food, or wealth, or grain, or riches of this kind, I would be in league with that evil man and would become his accomplice." Having thought this, he cut off the hunter's life.

"Having thus cut off the hunter's life, he took the lion's pelt and went to that mountain. There he piled up gośīrṣa sandalwood and cremated the lion's pelt together with its body. When he had thus cremated the lion's body, he gathered up the bones, built a stupa, and made offerings to it."

The Blessed One then said to Ānanda, "Sons of noble family, at that time, on that occasion, the lion called Firm Vow was none other than I myself, now Śākyamuni. Therefore, because he relies on a spiritual friend, a bodhisattva would sooner let body and life perish than give rise to an evil thought. Why is that? Because he knows kindness and repays kindness. And how is that so? By relying on a spiritual friend, a bodhisattva swiftly attains unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening.

"Son of noble family, a bodhisattva constantly and diligently relies on spiritual friends, and in order to conquer and crush the afflictions of the three realms, he strives earnestly to hear even a single word of the Buddha's Dharma, or a single verse, or even a single point of meaning. When a bodhisattva thus seeks the Buddha's teaching single-mindedly, he is so fully possessed by the longing for the Dharma that, not sparing even body and life, he would make his way across iron glowing with embers, or crawl along the very ground, without a thought for the harm. If a bodhisattva does not spare body and life even for the sake of a few verses, what need is there to speak of the twelve divisions of the scriptures? If he does not spare body and life even for a single verse, what need is there to speak of anything else, of outward material things?

"Through hearing the Dharma, his body and mind become deeply at ease, and as a mind of great faith arises, he attains the mind of right view. When he sees one who teaches the Dharma, it is as if he were seeing his father and mother, and no pride arises in him. He listens to the Dharma for the sake of sentient beings, not for gain and fame. He works for the benefit and happiness of sentient beings, not for his own benefit. For the sake of the true Dharma he has no fear of kings, of hunger and thirst, cold and heat, of tigers, wolves and other fierce beasts of prey, of thieves, enemies and the like. First he subdues the afflictions of the senses, and only then listens to the Dharma; he does not listen at the wrong time, but listens single-mindedly. Showing reverence to the Dharma, he serves the teacher of the Dharma with respect. This is called the bodhisattva's knowing kindness and repaying kindness.

"How does a bodhisattva listen to the Dharma single-mindedly? There are four ways of listening to the Dharma: with a true mind, with a one-pointed mind, with a mind given wholly in every respect, and with a virtuous mind. These four are called the bodhisattva's diligence in seeking the twelve divisions of the scriptures. Why is that? He keeps in mind the weighty kindness of the buddhas in order to spread the true Dharma of all the buddhas everywhere, in order to make the true Dharma of all the buddhas arise and increase, in order that worldly people may have faith in the Buddha's Dharma, and in order to bring all sentient beings to unsurpassed awakening. Therefore the bodhisattva keeps in mind the countless sentient beings of the ten directions. In order to repay the weighty kindness of all the buddhas, the bodhisattva strives to seek the twelve divisions of the scriptures.

"When a bodhisattva diligently seeks the Buddha's Dharma, he studies the treatises on the science of reasoning so that all sentient beings may give rise to faith.
